Introduction to C # and the .NET Framework

C # is an elegant, strongly typed, object-oriented language for developing a variety of secure and powerful applications that run in the [.NET Framework w ork](https://translate.googleusercontent.com/translate_f#_.NET_Framework_Conceptual) [[i]](https://translate.googleusercontent.com/translate_f#_edn1) . You can develop common Windows client applications, XML Web services, distributed components, server-client applications, database applications, and many, many others in C #. Visual C # 2008 includes an advanced code editor, user-friendly designers, a built-in debugger, and many other tools to help you develop applications in C # 3.0 and .NET Framework 3.5.

| ![Note](Image00001.jpg) Note. |
| Reading the Visual C # documentation assumes that the user has knowledge of basic programming principles. For absolute beginners, the Express Edition version of Visual C # is available on the Internet. You can also take advantage of C # books and resources on the Internet to gain hands-on programming skills. |

C # language

The C # syntax is very expressive, but easy to learn. Anyone familiar with C, C ++, or Java will easily recognize the curly brace syntax that is specific to the C # language. Developers with knowledge of any of these languages ​ ​ will generally be able to work effectively with the C # language in a very short time. C # syntax simplifies things that were difficult in C ++ and provides powerful capabilities such as Nullable value types, enums, delegates, lambda expressions, and direct memory access that Java does not. C # supports generic methods and types for greater security and performance, as well as iterators, which let you define your own iteration behavior when implementing class collections that can be easily used in client code. In C # 3.0, Language-Integrated Query (LINQ) expressions make a strongly typed query a first-class language construct.

As an object-oriented language, C # supports the concepts of encapsulation, inheritance, and polymorphism. All variables and methods, including the M ain method , the entry point of the application, are encapsulated in class definitions. A class can inherit directly from one parent class, but it can implement any number of interfaces. Methods that override virtual methods in the parent class require the override keyword to prevent accidental redefinition . In C #, a structure is similar to a lightweight class: it is a stack- allocated type that implements interfaces but does not support inheritance.

In addition to the basic object-oriented principles described, C # simplifies the development of software components through several innovative language constructs, including the following:

*   Encapsulated method signatures, called delegates , that support strongly typed event notifications.
*   Properties that act as accessors for private member variables.
*   Attributes with declarative type metadata at runtime.
*   Inline XML documentation comments.
*   LINQ (Language-Integrated Query), which offers built-in query capabilities across multiple data sources.

If you need to interoperate with other Windows software, such as COM objects or native Win32 DLLs, you can use a process in C # called "Interop." The Interop process allows C # programs to do just about any action that a native C ++ application might do. C # even supports pointers and the notion of "unsafe" code in cases where direct memory access is critical.

Compared to C and C ++, the build process for C # is simpler and more flexible than Java. There are no separate header files, and methods and types do not need to be declared in a specific order. Any number of classes, structures, interfaces, and events can be defined in a C # source file.

.NET Framework architecture

A C # program runs in the .NET Framework, an integrated Windows component that contains the virtual runtime (CLR) and a unified set of class libraries. The CLR is Microsoft's commercial implementation of the CLI, which is the international standard that underpins the creation of runtime and development environments that enable close interoperability between languages ​ ​ and libraries.

Source code written in C # is compiled into an intermediate language (IL) according to the CLI specification. IL code and resources such as bitmaps and strings are stored on disk in an executable file called an assembly, with an EXE or DLL extension in most cases. An assembly contains a manifest with information about assembly types, version, culture, and security requirements.

When you run a C # program, the assembly is loaded into the CLR based on the information in the manifest. Next, if the security requirements are met, the CLR performs JIT compilation to convert the IL code to machine code instructions. The CLR also provides other services related to automatic garbage collection, exception handling, and resource management. The code executed by the CLR is sometimes referred to as "managed code" as opposed to "unmanaged code", which is compiled into machine code that is intended for a specific system. The following shows the compile-time and run-time relationships between C # source files, .NET Framework class libraries, assemblies, and the CLR.

![From C # source code to machine execution](Image00002.jpg)

Interoperability between languages ​ ​ is a key feature of the .NET Framework. Because the IL code generated by the C # compiler conforms to the CTS specification, C #-based IL code can interoperate with code generated by the Visual Basic, Visual C ++, Visual J # versions of the .NET Framework, and over 20 other CTS-compatible languages. A single assembly can contain multiple modules written in different languages ​ ​ of the .NET Framework, and types can refer to each other as if they were written in the same language.

In addition to runtime services, the .NET Framework also has an extensive library of over 4,000 namespaced classes that provide a variety of useful functions for everything from file input and output to string manipulation for XML parsing, to Windows Forms controls. In a typical C # application, the .NET Framework class library is heavily used to "device" code.

Creating Your First C # Application

It only takes a minute to create a C # application. Follow these steps to create a program that opens a window and reacts to a button press.

To create a C # application

1.  On the File menu, point to New , and then click Project .
2.  Ensure the Windows Application template is selected, in the Name field, type MyProject , and click OK .

You will see a Windows Form in the Windows Forms designer. This is the user interface for your application.

3.  On the View menu, click Toolbox to make the list of controls visible.
4.  Expand the Common Controls list, and drag the Label control to your form.
5.  Also from the Toolbox Common Controls list, drag a button onto the form, near the label.
6.  Double-click the new button to open the Code Editor. Visual C # has inserted a method called button1_Click that is executed when the button is clicked.
7.  Change the method to look like this:

| private void button1_Click (object sender, EventArgs e){    label1.Text = "Hello, World!";} |

8.  Press F5 to compile and run your application.

When you click the button, a text message is displayed. Congratu lations! You've just written your first C # application.

Create your first C # application

It takes less than a minute to create a C # application. Follow the steps below to create a program that opens a window and responds to a button click.

Create a C # Application

1.  On the File menu, point to New and click Project .
2.  Make sure the Windows Application template is selected , type MyProject in the Name field, and click OK .

The Windows Form Designer opens with a Windows Form. This is the user interface for the application you are creating.

3.  On the View menu, click Toolbox to open the list of controls.
4.  Expand the Standard Controls list and drag a Signature control onto the form.
5.  Also, from the list of the Standard Controls toolbox, drag a button to the form and place it next to the signature.
6.  Double click the new button to open the code editor. Visual C # has inserted a method named button1_Click that is executed when the button is clicked.
7.  Change the method as follows.

| private void button1_Click (object sender, EventArgs e){    label1.Text = "Hello, World!";} |

8.  Press the F5 key to compile and run the application.

Clicking on the button will now display a text message. Congratulations ! The first C # application is created .

To create a C # console application

The purpose of this topic is to acquaint you with the Visual C # Express Edition development environment as you build the simplest form of C # program, a console application. Because console applica tions perform all their input and output at the command line, they are ideal for quickly trying out language features and writing command-line utilities.

| The features of the development environment discussed in this section will also be encountered when you develop Windows Forms applications. Don't skip this part just because you don't plan on writing console applications! |

In this section, you'll learn:

*   How to create a new console application.
*   How to use bookmarks in the Code editor.
*   How to view Solution Explorer.
*   How to keep your code nicely formatted.
*   How to use IntelliSense to make entering code faster and more accurate.
*   How to build and run your application.

The program that you create in this task uses the classes in the System.IO namespace to obtain and display a list of files and file sizes in the C: \ directory. You could use this code as a basis for a utility that searches a directory for a particular file name.

1.  On the File menu, click New Project .

The New Project dialog box appears. This dialog box lists the different default application types that Visual C # Express Edition can create.

2.  Select Console Application as your project type and change the name of your application to List Files .

The default location should be fine, but you can always enter a new path if you want.

3.  Click OK .

Create a C # console application

The purpose of this section is to introduce you to the Visual C # Express Edition development environment by building the simplest form of a C # program, a console application. Because console applications perform input and output through the command line, they are ideal for quickly becoming familiar with the language's capabilities and writing command line utilities.

| The development environment capabilities described in this section will also be used when developing Windows Forms applications. Even if you don't plan on building console apps, don't skip this part! |

Content

*   Creating a new console application.
*   Using bookmarks in the code editor.
*   Using Solution Explorer.
*   Maintaining correct code formatting.
*   Use IntelliSense to write code faster and more accurately.
*   Building and running the application

The program you create in this section uses classes in the System.IO namespace to get and display a list of the names and sizes of files in the C: directory. This code can be used as the basis for utilities to search a directory for a file with a specific name.

1.  On the File menu, choose New Project .

The New Project dialog box opens . This dialog box lists the different types of default applications that you can build with Visual C # Express.

2.  Select Console Application as the project type and change the application name to File List . You can leave the default location or specify a new path as you like.
3.  Click the OK button .

Visual C # Express Edition creates a new folder for your project named after the project title. It also opens the main Visual C # Express Edition window and the Code pane where you will enter and mo dify the C # source code that creates your application. 

![ExpressCS screenshot](Image00003.jpg)

Notice the toolbar at the top of the window. This toolbar contains icons for creating, loading and saving projects, editing source code, building your application, and hiding and displaying other windows that make up the Visual C # Express Edition environment. The five icons at the far right of this toolbar are used to open important windows such as Solution Explorer and the Toolbox . Place the mouse pointer over any one of these icons to obtain pop-up tooltip Help.

| Bookmarks are useful when you write large programs, as they enable you to quickly jump from one location in the source code to another. To create a bookmark, click the Toggle bookmark icon, or press CTRL + B, T. You see a cyan marker in the margin. Use the same procedure to delete an existing bookmark. You can create as many bookmarks as you like, and jump between them using the Next and Previous Bookmark icons, or by pressing CTRL + B, N, and CTRL + B, P. |

Visual C # Express will create a new folder for the project with the same name as the project. This will open the main Visual C # window, Express Edition, and the Code Panel for entering and modifying the source code of your C # application. 

![ExpressCS screenshot](Image00003.jpg)

Pay attention to the toolbar at the top of the window. The toolbar contains icons for creating, loading and saving projects, editing source code, building an application, hiding and showing other windows that are part of the Visual C # Express Edition environment. On the right edge of the toolbar are five icons for opening important windows such as Solution Explorer and Toolbox . When you hover the mouse over any of these icons, a tooltip appears.

| Bookmarks are useful when writing large programs, with their help you can quickly jump from one place in the source code to another. To create a bookmark, you need to click the "Bookmark" icon or press the key combination CTRL + B, T. A blue marker will appear on the field. To delete an existing bookmark, follow the same procedure. You can create any number of bookmarks and move between them using the Next Bookmark and Previous Bookmark icons or by pressing CTRL + B, N and CTRL + B, P. |

4.  Make sure that Solution Explorer is visible by clic king the Solution Explorer tab on the right of the screen, or the Solution Explorer icon in the toolbar.

Solution Explorer is a very useful pane as it displays the various files that make up your project. The most important file in this project is the file "Program.cs," which contains the source code for your application.

![ExpressThumbtack](Image00004.jpg)

Knowing how to open and hide windows like Solution Explorer is important if you want to keep your Visual C # Express Edition display nice and tidy. By default, Solution Explorer is visible. If you want to hide Solution Explorer , click the Auto Hide icon, the push-pin icon in its title bar, or open the Options menu on the title bar of Solution Explorer and enable Auto Hide . Other windows, such as Class View and Properties , also have these icons. 

5.  Type the class name Console into the Code Editor .

If Solution Explorer is still obscuring the Code pane, click in the Code pane to hide it. Now click to the right of the open curly brace ({) inside the Main method, and press ENTER to start a new line. Notice how the editor automatically indents the cursor.

| The Code Editor always tries to keep your code formatted in a standard, easy-to-read layout. If your code ever starts to look messy, you can reformat all of the document by clicking Advanced , and then Format Document from the Edit menu, or by pressing CTRL + E, D. |

4.  Verify that Solution Explorer is displayed by clicking the Solution Explorer tab on the right side of the screen or the Solution Explorer icon in the toolbar.

Panel Solution Explorer is very useful, it is displayed on the various files that are part of the project. The most important project file is "Program.cs", which contains the source code for the application.

![ExpressThumbtack](Image00005.jpg)

It is important to know how to open and hide windows like Solution Explorer so that Visual C # Express Edition will be kept as it should be. Solution Explorer is visible by default. To hide Solution Explorer, click the Auto-Hide icon (a pushpin icon in the title bar), or open the Options menu in the Solution Explorer title bar and select Auto Hide . Other windows such as Class View and Properties also have these icons.

5.  Enter the class name Console in the code editor .

If Solution Explorer still hides the Code pane, click the Code pane to hide it. Now click the right open curly brace ({) inside the Main method and press Enter to move to a new line. Note that the editor indents automatically.

| The code editor always tries to format the entered code in a standard, easy-to-read structure. If the code looks unattractive, you can reformat the entire document by choosing More and Format Document from the Edit menu, or by pressing CTRL + E, D. |

When you type a C # class name or keyword, you have a choice: you can either type the complete word yourself, or let the IntelliSense tool that is part of the Code pane do it for you. For example, when you type a "c", a pop- up list of words appears as IntelliSense tries to predict the word you are typing. In this case, you won't see the word "Console" displayed just yet. Either scroll down the list, or continue typing the word "console." When "console" appears highlighted in the list, press ENTER, or TAB, or double-click it, and Console will be added to your code.

![ExpressCase screenshot](Image00006.jpg)

The advantage of using IntelliSense is that you can be certain the casing and the spelling are correct. Whether you type the code or let IntelliSense do it is comp letely up to you.

When you enter a class name or a C # keyword, you have the choice to either finish typing the word yourself, or let the built-in IntelliSense tool in the Code pane do so . For example, typing "c" will display a pop-up list of words suggested by IntelliSense from which you can select the word you are typing. In this case, the word "Console" is not yet visible. Either scroll down the list or keep typing the word "console". When the word "console" is highlighted in the list, press Enter, or TAB, or double-click it, Console will be added to the code.

![ExpressCase screenshot](Image00006.jpg) The advantage of using IntelliSense is that you are confident in the correct case and spelling of the word. The user can either enter the code himself or use IntelliSense technology.

6.  Type a period and the method name WriteLine .

As soon as you type the period after Console , another IntelliSense list is displayed. This list contains all the possible methods and properties that are part of the Console class. You want the WriteLine method, and you can see it at the bottom of the list. Either finish typing WriteLine yourself, or press the DOWN ARROW key until it is selected, and press ENTER, or TAB, or double-click it. WriteLine will be added to your code.

![ExpressConsole](Image00007.jpg)

Type an opening parenthesis. You'll immediately see another IntelliSense feature, the method signatures, appear as a tool tip message. In this case you can see there are 19 different signatures, and you can look through them by clicking the UP and DOWN ARROW keys.

7.  Type the string "This program lists all the files in the directory . "

Type the message enclosed in quotation marks and add a closing parenthesis. You'll see a red wavy underline displayed as a reminder that something is missing. Type a semicolon (;) and the underline will disappear.

8.  Finish the program.

Type or copy and paste the following code to complete the program:

static void Main ( string [] args)

{

    Console.WriteLine ( "This program lists all the files in the directory:" );

    System.IO.DirectoryInf o dir = new System.IO.DirectoryInfo (@ "C: \" );

    foreach (System.IO.FileInfo file in dir.GetFiles ( "*. *" ))

    {

        Console.WriteLine ( "{0}, {1}" , file.Name, file.Length);

    }

    Console.ReadLine ();

}

6.  Enter a period and a name for the WriteLine method .

As soon as the user enters a period after the Console , IntelliSense displays a different list. The list contains the names of all possible methods and properties that are part of the Console class . The required WriteLine method can be seen at the end of the list. Either finish typing W riteLine , or press the DOWN ARROW key to select, then Enter, or TAB, or double-click it. The WriteLine method will be added to the code.

![ExpressConsole](Image00008.jpg)

Enter an open parenthesis. A tooltip message appears with method signatures, which is another IntelliSense feature. In this case, 19 different labels will be displayed, which can be viewed using the UP ARROW and DOWN ARROW keys.

7.  Enter the line "This program displays a list of all files in the directory . "

Type your message in single quotes and add a closing parenthesis. A red wavy underline will appear to remind you that something is missing. Enter a semicolon (;) and the underline will disappear.

8.  End the program.

Enter or paste the following code to complete the program.

static void Main ( string [] args)

{

    Console.WriteLine ( "This program lists all the files in the directory:" );

    System.IO.DirectoryInfo dir = new System.IO.DirectoryInfo (@ "C: \" );

    foreach (System.IO.FileInfo file in dir.GetFiles ( "*. *" ) )

    {

        Console.WriteLine ( "{0}, {1}" , file.Name, file.Length);

    }

    Console.ReadLine ();

}

The last line in the program is Console.ReadLine (); which causes the program to pause until ENTER is pressed. If you omit this line, the command-line window will immediately disappear and you won't be able to see the output of your program. If you are creating a command-line utility that will always be used from the command-line console, you will probably want to omit the call to the ReadLine () method.

*   Run your program.

Your first program is now complete and ready to compile and run. To do this, either press F5 or click on the Start icon in the toolbar.

![VJS Express List Files Start](Image00009.jpg)

*   Once the program compiles and runs, the Console window opens and a list of files and their sizes is displayed. Press ENTER to exit the program.

The last line in the program Console.ReadLine (); is designed to pause the program execution until you press the ENTER key. If you do not add this line, the command line window will disappear immediately and the user will not be able to see the output of the execution results. If the generated utility will always be used in the command line console, you can omit the call to the ReadLine () method .

*   Run the program.

The program is now complete, ready for compilation and execution. To do this, either press the F5 key or click the Start icon in the toolbar.

![Running VJS Express List Files](Image00009.jpg)

*   After compilation and execution, the Console window will open with a list of files and their sizes. Press Enter to exit the program.

To create a C # Windows application

The purpose of this topic i s to acquaint you with elements of the Visual C # Express Edition integrated development environment (IDE) as you use Windows Forms to build a relatively straightforward C # program. Windows Forms provide your project with components, such as dialog boxes, menus, buttons, and many other controls, that make up a standard Windows application user interface (UI). Fundamentally, these controls are just classes from the .NET Framework class library. The Designer view in Visual C # Express Edition enables you to drag the controls onto your application's main form and adjust their size and position. As you do this, the IDE automatically adds the source code to create an instance of the appropriate class and initialize it. 

This example shows you how to create your own Web browser application, which you can customize with shortcuts to your favorite Web sites.

In this section, you'll learn how to complete the following tasks:

*   Create a new Windows Forms application.
*   Toggle between Code view and Designer view.
*   Change the Windows Form's properties.
*   Add a MenuStrip control.
*   Add a Button control.
*   Create and populate a ComboBox control.
*   Use a WebBrowser control.
*   Create event handlers for controls.

1.  On the File menu, click New Project .

The New Project dialog box appears. This dialog box lists the different default application types that Visual C # Express Edition can create.

2.  Select Windows Forms Application as your project type.
3.  Change the name of your application to Web Browser .
4.  Click OK .

Building Windows Forms Applications in C #

The purpose of this section is to introduce you to the elements of the Visual C # Development Environment, Express Edition, by using Windows Forms to build a fairly simple C # program. Windows Forms provides components such as dialog boxes, menus, buttons, and many other controls for a project that are part of the standard Windows user interface (UI). Essentially, these controls are just classes from the .NET Framework library. Designer view in Visual C # Express Edition lets you drag and drop controls onto the main form of your application and change their size and position. The IDE will then automatically add the source code to create and initialize an instance of the corresponding class.

This example shows you how to create a web browser application that you can customize with links to your favorite Web sites.

This section covers the following tasks.

*   Create a new W indows Forms application.
*   Switch between Code view and Design view .
*   Change Windows Form Properties.
*   Adding a MenuStrip control .
*   Adding a Button control .
*   Creation and filling of the Comb o Box control .
*   Using the WebBrow control s er .
*   Creating event handlers for controls.

1.  On the File menu, choose New Project .

The New Project dialog box opens . This dialog box lists the different types of default applications that you can build with Visual C # Express.

2.  Select Windows Forms Application as the project type.
3.  Change the application name to Web Browser .
4.  Click OK .

Visual C # Express Edition creates a new folder for your project tha t is named after the project title, and then displays your new Windows Form, titled Form1 in Designer view. You can switch between this view and Code view at any time by right-clicking the design surface or code window and then clicking View Code or View Designer . 

![ExpressForm1cs screenshot](Image00010.jpg)

The Windows Form you see in Designer view is a visual representation of the window that will open when your application is opened. In Designer view, you can drag various controls from the Toolbox onto the form. These controls are not really "live"; they are just images that are convenient to move around on the form into a precise location.

After you have dropped a control onto the form, Visual C # works behind the scenes to create the code that will cause the real control to be positioned correctly when the program is run. This source code is in a file that is generally nested out of view. You can see this file in Solution Explorer , which is named Form1.designer.cs, if you expand Form1.cs.

5.  If you are in Code view, switch to Designer view by right-clicking the code window and then clicking View Designer . Now change the size of the Windows Form.
    1.  Click the lower-right corner of the Windows Form.
    2.  When the pointer becomes a double-headed arrow, drag the corner of the form until it is at least as wide and as deep as a quarter of your screen.

Because this is the window in which Web pages will be displayed, you don't want it to be too cramped.

Visual C # Express will create a new folder for the project with the same name as the project, and then display a new Windows form named From1 in Design view . You can switch between this view and the Code view by right-clicking the design surface and choosing View Code or Open in Designer .

![Screenshot of ExpressForm1cs](Image00011.jpg)

A Windows Form in Design View is a visual representation of a window that appears when you open an application. In Design view, you can drag and drop various controls from the Toolbox onto your form. These controls are not real objects, they are just images that can be conveniently moved around the form to a specific location.

Once the control is positioned on the form, Visual C # will generate code in the background to position the real control correctly when the program runs. This source code will be in a file that is not normally visible in the view. This file, named Form1.designer.cs, can be seen in Solution Explorer when you expand the Form1.cs node.

5.  You can switch from Code view to Design view by right-clicking the code window and choosing Open in Design . Resizing Windows Forms.
    1.  Click the lower right corner of the Windows Form.
    2.  When the pointer changes to a double-headed arrow, drag the corner so that the shape fills a quarter of the screen.

Since this window will display web pages, it should not be too small.

6.  Make sure the Properties window is displayed. Its default location is the lower-right section of the IDE, but you can move to another location if you like. The following illustration shows it in the upper-right corner. If you do not see the Properties window, on the View menu, click Properties window. This window lists the properties of the currently selected Windows Form or control, and it's here that you can change the existing values.
7.  Change the title of the Windows Form.
    1.  Click on the form to select it.
    2.  In the Properties window, scroll down to Text , select the text " Form1 ," and type Web Browser .
    3.  Press ENTER or TAB to move focus from the "Text" text box.

You now see that the text at the top of your Windows Form (in the area called the title bar) has changed.

![ExpressTextWebBrowser screenshot](Image00012.jpg)

To quickly change the name of a control, right-click the control and click Properties . You can type the new name for the control in the Name property.

6.  Make sure the Properties window is open. By default, it is located at the bottom right side of the IDE, but can be moved to any location. The following illustration shows it in the upper right corner. If the Properties window is not visible, choose Properties from the View menu . This window lists the properties of the currently selected Windows form or control, here you can change their values.
7.  Change the name of the Windows form.
    1.  Click a shape to select it.
    2.  In the Properties window, scroll down to Text , select the text " Form1 " and enter Web Browser .
    3.  Press Enter or Tab to remove focus from the Text text box.

The text at the top of the Windows Form (in the area called the title bar) has changed.

![Screenshot ExpressTextWebBrowser](Image00012.jpg) To quickly change the name of a control, right-click it and select Properties . Enter a new name for the control in the Name property .

8.  Click the Toolbox button on th e toolbar, or on the View menu, click Toolbox . Scroll down the list of controls and expand Menus & Toolbars until you see MenuStrip. Drag this control to anywhere on the Windows Form.

![ExpressMainmenuForm screenshot](Image00013.jpg)

This control creates a default menu at the top of the form.

8.  Click the Toolbox button on the toolbar, or on the View menu, select Toolbox . Scroll down the list of controls and expand the Menus and Toolbars node until you see the MenuStrip . Drag this control anywhere on the Windows Form.

![Screenshot of ExpressMainmenuForm](Image00014.jpg)

This control creates a default menu at the top of the form.

9.  In the box that says Type Here , type the n ame of the menu, in this case Navigate . When you press ENTER, new empty boxes appear to create other menus, and menu items. In the lower box, type Home . Press ENTER and more boxes are displayed. Type Go Back . Press ENTER, and type Go Forward .

![ExpressTypeHere screenshot](Image00015.jpg)

These menu items form your basic Web site navigation controls.

10.  Add a button.

From the Toolbox , in the Common Controls category, drag a Button control to approximately the middle of the Windows Form, just under the menu bar. In the Properties window, change the Text p roperty to Go instead of button1 , and change the design name, which resembles (Name) , from button1 to goButton .

9.  In the box with the text Enter Place, enter a name for the menu, in this case Navigate . When you press Enter, new fields appear to create other menus and menu items. In the box below, enter Home Page . Press Enter and additional fields will appear. Enter Back . Press Enter and type Forward .

![Screenshot ExpressTypeHere](Image00015.jpg)

These menu items are the main controls for navigating a Web site.

10.  Adding a button.

From the Standard Toolbars category, drag the Button control to about the middle of the Windows Form, just below the menu bar. In the Properties window, change the Text property to Go instead of button1, and change the template name in the (Name) property from button1 to goButton .

11.  Add a ComboBox .

From the Toolbox , in the Common Controls category, drag a ComboBox contr ol and position it to the left of the new button. Drag the edges and corners to resize and reposition the ComboBox until it is lined up with the button.

| ![Note](Image00001.jpg) Note: |
| When you are moving controls around a Windows Form, you will see blue lines appear. These lines are guides that help you line up the controls vertically and horizontally. You can also line up controls by selecting more than one at a time. You can do this by clicking and dragging a selection box around the controls or by holding down SHIFT as you click them. After you have multiple controls selected, you can modify the alignment and size by using the align and resize icons. These icons appear on the Layout Toolbar at the top of the Design window. |

12.  Populate the ComboBox.

A ComboBox provides a drop-down list of options from which the user can select. In this program, the ComboBox will contain a list of your favorite Web sites for quick access.

To create the list of sites, select the ComboBox and view its properties. Select the Items property, and you'll see the word (Collection) with an ellipsis button (...) . Click this button to change the contents of the ComboBox. Add as many Web site URLs as you want, pressing RETURN after each.

11.  Adding a ComboBox control .

From the Standard Toolbars category, drag a ComboBox control and position it to the left of the new button. By dragging the edges and corners, resize the item and position the ComboBox in line with the button.

| ![](Image00001.jpg) Note. |
| Blue lines appear when you move controls on a Windows Form. They are guides for vertically and horizontally aligning elements. In addition, it is possible to align several elements at the same time by selecting them. To do this, click the shape and drag the selection rectangle around the desired items, or click each of them while holding down the SHIFT key. Once you have selected multiple items, you can align and resize them using the align and resize icons. These icons appear in the Layout toolbar at the top of the design window . |

12.  Populating the ComboBox control .

The ComboBox control provides a drop-down list of choices. In this program, the ComboBox will contain a list of your favorite Web sites for quick access.

To create a list of Web sites, select the ComboBox and display its properties. Selecting the Elements property displays a button with an ellipsis (...) and the word (Collection) . Click this button to change the contents of the ComboBox . Add any number of Web site URLs by pressing ENTER after each.

| ![](Image00001.jpg) Note.Make sure to insert http: // in front of each website address . |

13.  Add the WebBrowser control.

From the Toolbox , in the Common Controls category, scroll down until you locate the WebBrowser control. Drag the control to the Windows Form. Resize the WebBrowser control to fit inside the Windows Form without obscuring the ComboBox and Button controls. If the WebBrowser control doesn't resize easily, first set it to the desired size, open its properties, locate the Dock setting, and make sure that it is set to none . Setting the Anchor settings to Top, Bottom, Left, Right will cause the WebBrowser control to resize correctly when you resize the application window.

The WebBrowser control is the control that does all the hard work of rendering the Web pages. You access it in your application through an instance of the WebBrowser class. View form1.Designer.cs and you will see that an instance of this class has been added to your application code, together with instances of classes that represent the other items that you added by using the designer. It is these instances that you will be using when you add event handlers for, and call methods on, the controls.

14.  Add an event handler for the Button control.

You have now finished the design stage of your application and are at the point when you can start adding some code to provide the program's functionality.

The program must have event handlers for the button and for each menu option. An event handler is a method that is executed when the user interacts with the control. Visual C # Express Edition creates empty event handlers for you automatically.

Double-click the button, and you'll see the Code Editor for your project appear. You'll also see that the event handler for the click event, which is the event message that occurs when the user clicks a button, has been created for you. Add code to the event handler method so that it resembles the following code.

private void goButton _ Click ( object sender , System . EventArgs e )

{

    webBrowser1.Navigate ( new Uri (comboBox1.SelectedItem.ToString ()));

}

13.  Adding a WebBrowser control .

In the Toolbox , under the Standard category, scroll down the list to the WebBrowser control . Drag this control onto a Windows Form. Resize the WebBrowser to fill the Windows Form without overlapping the ComboBox and Button controls . If resizing the WebBrowser control is difficult, open its properties, find the Dock option and make sure it is set to none , then set the size you want. Setting parameter Anchor value from the top, bottom, left and right will make the control WebBrowser properly resized when you resize the application window.

The WebBrowser element handles all the complex work of rendering web pages. In the application, it is accessed through an instance of the WebBrowser class . Viewing the form1.Designer.cs file will show that an instance of this class has been added to the application code, along with class instances representing other elements added through the designer. These are the instances that will be used when adding event handlers and calling control methods.

14.  Add an event handler for the Button control .

At this point, the application design phase is over, it's time to add some code to provide the program's functionality.

The program must have event handlers for the buttons and each menu item. An event handler is a method that is executed when a user interacts with a control. Visual C # Express automatically generates empty event handlers.

Double click the button to open the code editor. You will see that the environment has created an event handler for the Click event that occurs when the user clicks the button . Add the following code to your event handler method.

private void goButton _ Click ( object sender , System . EventArgs e )

{

    webBrowser1.Navigate ( new Uri (comboBox1.SelectedItem.ToString ()));

}

This code takes the currently selec ted item from the ComboBox control, a string that contains a Web URL, and passes it to the Web browser's Navigate method. The Navigate method loads and displays the contents of the Web page at that location.

15.  Add event handlers for the MenuStrip options.

Return to the Designer window and double-click each sub-item in the menu in turn. Visual C # Express Edition creates event handler methods for each. Edit these methods so that they resemble the following code. 

| private void homeToolStripMenuItem_Click (object sender, System.EventArgs e){    webBrowser1.GoHome ();}private void goForwardToolStripMenuItem_Click (object sender, System.EventArgs e){    webBrowser1.GoForward ();}private void goBackToolStripMenuItem_Click (object sender, System.EventArgs e){    webBrowser1.GoBack ();} |

Each of these menu handlers calls a navigation method that is supported on the WebBrowser class.

This code takes the currently selected item from the ComboBox control (the string containing the URL of the Web site) and passes it to the Web browser's Navigate method . The Navigate method loads and displays the content of the specified web page.

15.  Add an event handler for the MenuStrip parameters .

Return to the design window and double-click each menu item in the menu in turn. Visual C # Express will create event handler methods for each. Modify these methods , both indicated below .

private void homeToolStr ipMenuItem_Click (object sender, System.EventArgs e)

{

    webBrowser1.GoHome ();

}

private void goForwardToolStripMenuItem_Click (object sender, System.EventArgs e)

{

    webBrowser1.GoForward ();

}

private void goBackToolStripMenuItem_Click (object sender, System.EventArgs e)

{

    webBrowser1.GoBack ();

}

Each of these menu handlers calls a navigation method supported by the WebBrowser class .

| You can see from this code that the default names given to the menu options can become very confusing. For this reason, it's a good idea to change the name of e ach menu control as you create it by using the Properties editor. The name of the handler will then reflect the name of the menu option. |

16.  See the code that Visual C # has written for you.

The Visual C # IDE has already written initialization code for you. In Code view, find the constructor for the Form1 class. It has a signature of public Form1 () . Right-click the InitializeComponent method that is being called from inside the constructor, and then click Go To Definition . You now see all the code that was being written behind the scenes as you were dragging and dropping controls and setting properties in the Properties window.

17.  Add some initialization code of your own.

The last task is to add some initialization code of your own to Form1 . The constructor should never be used to call any code that might throw an exception. Therefore, any such code must be located someplace else, and that location is the Form1_Load method. Click the Form1.cs [Design] tab at the top of the code editor to go back to Windows Form. Select the form and in the Properties window click the Events button (the one with the lightning bolt) and then double-click Load . This will add an event handler method and position your cursor in the method in Code view.

When a user starts your program, Windows will notify your application's form by sending a Load event. When the form receives that event, it will call the Form1_Load method. Methods that are called in response to events are called event handlers. The system will call your event at the appropriate time; your job is to put the code into the event handler that you want to execute when the event occurs.

In Code view, add two lines to the Form1_Load method as shown in the following code. This will cause the WebBrowser control to display your computer's default home page and also set the initial value of the ComboBox.

| It is clear from this code that using the default names for the menu options can get quite confusing. For this reason, it is best to change the name of each menu option using the Properties editor . Then the name of the handler will reflect the name of the menu option. |

16.  View generated Visual C # code.

The Visual C # IDE automatically generates initialization code. In Code view, find the constructor for the Form1 class . It has the signature public Form1 () . Right-click the InitializeComponent method called from the constructor and choose Go To Definition . This will show the code generated by the background process while dragging and dropping controls and setting properties in the Properties window .

17.  Adding your own initialization code.

Finally, let's add our own initialization code to Form1 . The constructor should never be used to call any code that might throw an exception. Such code should be located in the Form1_Load method . Click the Form1.cs [Design] tab at the top of the code editor to return to the Windows Form. Select the form and in the Properties window, click the Events button (with a lightning bolt), then double-click Load . The event handler will be created , and the cursor moves to the method in the code .

When the program starts, Windows will notify the application form of this by dispatching the Load event . When the form receives this event, the Form1_Load method will be called . Methods that are called in response to an event are called event handlers. The system will raise events at the right moment in time, the developer's task is to put code in the event handler that should be executed when an event occurs.

In Code view, add two lines to the Form1_Load method as shown in the code below. This will cause the WebBrowser control to display the default home page and assign an initial value to the ComboBox control .

| private void Form1_Load (object sender, EventArgs e){    comboBox1.SelectedIndex = 0;    webBrowser1.GoHome ();} |

18.  Build and run the program.

Press F5 to build and run the Web browser. The Windows Form is displayed on the screen, and it then displays your computer's default home page. You can use the ComboBox control to select a Web site, and click Go to navigate to it. The menu options enable you to return home, or move back and forth through previously visited Web sites.

![MSN sample screenshot](Image00016.jpg)

 -------

18.  Building and executing a program.

Press F5 to build and run your web browser. The Windows Form will appear on the screen and then the default home page will appear. You can use the ComboBox control to select a Web site and the Go button to navigate to it. You can use the menu items to return to your home page or navigate back and forth through previously opened Web sites.

![Sample MSN Screenshot](Image00017.jpg)

Introduction to Windows Presentation Foundation

Windows Presentation Foundation (WPF) is a next- generation presentation system for building Windows client applications with visually stunning user experiences. With WPF, you can create a wide range of both standalone and browser-hosted applications. Some examples are [Yahoo!](https://translate.google.com/translate?hl=ar&prev=_t&sl=ru&tl=en&u=http://go.microsoft.com/fwlink/%3FLinkId%3D91192) [Messenger](https://translate.google.com/translate?hl=ar&prev=_t&sl=ru&tl=en&u=http://go.microsoft.com/fwlink/%3FLinkId%3D91192) and the [New York Times Reader](https://translate.google.com/translate?hl=ar&prev=_t&sl=ru&tl=en&u=http://go.microsoft.com/fwlink/%3FLinkId%3D91193) , as well as the [Contoso Healthcare Sample Application](https://translate.google.com/translate?hl=ar&prev=_t&sl=ru&tl=en&u=http://go.microsoft.com/fwlink/%3FLinkId%3D91196) that is shown in the following figure.   

![Contoso Healthcare UI sample](Image00018.jpg)

The core of WPF is a resolution-independent and vector-based rendering engine that is built to take advantage of modern graphics hardware. WPF extends the core with a comprehensive set of application-development features that include Extensible Application Markup Language (XAML), controls, data binding, layout, 2-D and 3-D graphics, animation, styles, templates, documents, media, text , and typography. WPF is included in the Microsoft .NET Framework, so you can build applications that incorporate other elements of the .NET Framework class library.         

To create a WPF application

The purpose of this topic is to show you how to create a simple Windows Presentation Foundation (WPF) application and become familiar with the Visual C # Express Edition integrated development environment (IDE). Like Windows Forms applications, WPF applications can be designed by dragging controls from the Toolbox to a design surface. In addition to having a designer, Properties window, and Toolbox , the IDE in WPF projects has a window that contains XAML. XAML is an acronym for Extensible Application Markup Language, which is used to create a user interface. The following illustration shows the location of the XAML editor.  

XAML editor

![XAML Window](Image00019.jpg)

This example shows you how to create your own Ink application, which enables you to draw pictures.

In this section, you'll learn how to:

*   Create a WPF application.
*   Toggle between Code view and Designer view.
*   Change the WPF window's properties.

Creating a WPF C # Application

The purpose of this section is to demonstrate how to create a simple Windows Presentation Foundation (WPF) application and introduce you to the Visual C # IDE Express Edition. Like Windows Forms applications, WPF applications can be created by dragging and dropping controls from the Toolbox onto the design surface. In addition to the designer, Properties window, and toolbox, the IDE in WPF projects has a XAML window. XAML is an acronym for Extensible Application Markup Language, the XAML language that is used to create the user interface. The following figure shows the location of the XAML editor.

![XAML Window](Image00019.jpg)

This example demonstrates how to create your own ink application that allows you to draw.

This section covers the following tasks.

*   Create a WPF Application.
*   Switch between Code view and Design view .
*   Change WPF window properties.

*   Use the XAML editor.

*   Add an System.Windows.Controls.InkCanvas control.
*   Add a System.Windows.Controls.Button control.
*   Create event handlers for controls.

1.  On the File menu, click New Project .

The New Project dialog box appears. This dialog box lists the different default application types that Visual C # Express Edition can create.

2.  Click WPF Application .
3.  Change the name of your application to Ink Pad .
4.  Click OK .

Visual C # Express Edition creates a new folder for your project that is named after the project title, and then displays your new WPF window, titled Window1, in Designer view. You can change to Code view at any time by right-clicking the designer surface or code window and selecting View Code . By default, the XAML editor is displayed under the designer, but you can see the XAML markup in full-screen mode by right-clicking the designer surface and clicking View XAML .  

The WPF window you see in Designer view is a visual representation of the window that will open when your application is started. In Designer view, you can drag various controls from the Toolbox onto the WPF window. After you have dropped a control onto the WPF window, Visual C # automatically creates code that will cause the control to be positioned appropriately when the program is run.

5.  If you cannot see the Properties window, on the View menu, click PropertiesWindow . This window lists the properties of the currently selected WPF window or control, and it's here that you can change the existing values.
6.  Change the size of the WPF window by setting the Height property to 550 and the Width property to 370 in the P roperties window.
7.  Change the title of the WPF window to Ink Pad .

*   Using the XAML editor.
*   Adding the System.Windows.Controls.InkCanvas control .
*   Adding the System.Windows.Controls.Button control .
*   Creating event handlers for controls.

1.  On the File menu, choose New Project .

The New Project dialog box opens . This dialog box lists the different types of default applications that you can build with Visual C # Express .

2.  Select WPF Application .
3.  Change the app name to Ink Pad .
4.  Click OK .

Visual C # Express creates a new folder for the project with the same name as the project, and then displays a new Windows window named Window1 in Design view . You can switch to Code view at any time by right-clicking a design surface or code window and choosing Code View . By default, the XAML editor appears below the designer, but XAML markup can also be viewed in full screen mode. To do this, right-click the design surface and select View XAML . 

A WPF Form in Design View is a visual representation of a window that opens when the application starts. In Design view, you can drag and drop various controls from the Toolbox into the WPF window. Once the control is positioned in the WPF window, Visual C # will automatically generate code to position the control correctly when the program runs.

5.  If the Properties window is not visible, on the View menu, select Properties Window . This window lists the properties of the currently selected WPF window or control, and you can also change existing values ​ ​ here.
6.  Resize the WPF window by setting the Height property to 550 and the Width property to 370 in the Properties window .
7.  Change the WPF window title to Ink Pad .

8.  Change the Background property of the WPF window to the color brown by clicking Brown in the drop-down box, and then pressing Enter.

| You can altern atively modify the XAML markup directly by adding a Background attribute and setting its value to Background = "Brown" . |

9.  To open the Toolbox , click the View menu, and then click Toolbox .
10.  Right-click the Toolbox , and then click Choose Items .

The Choose Toolbox Items dialog box opens.

11.  In the WPF Components tab of the Choose Toolbox Items dialog box, scroll down to InkCanvas , and select it so that a check appears in the check box.
12.  Click OK to add the InkCanvas control to the Toolbox .
13.  Drag an InkCanvas control from the Toolbox to the WPF window.
14.  Set the following properties of the InkCanvas control in the Properties window:

| Property | Value |
| Width | Auto |
| Height | Auto |
| HorizontalAlignment | Stretch |
| VerticalAlignment | Stretch |
| Margin | 9, 9, 9, 68 |

15.  Change the color of the InkCanvas control to yellow by setting its Background property to LightYellow .

The background color of the InkCanvas control will appear light yellow at run-time.

16.  Drag two Button controls to the WPF Window under the InkCanvas . Position button1 to the left and button2 to the right.

8.  Change the Background property of the WPF window to brown by clicking Brown in the drop-down list and pressing Enter.

| You can also directly modify the XAML markup by adding a Background attribute and setting its value to Background = "Brown" . |

9.  To open the Toolbox , on the View menu, select Toolbox .
10.  Right-click the Toolbox and select Select Elements . [[1]](https://translate.googleusercontent.com/translate_f#_ftn1)

The Select Toolbox Items dialog box opens .

11.  On the Components WPF dialog box Choose Toolbox Items , scroll down to the option InkCanvas and select it by checking the box.
12.  Click OK to add the InkCanvas control to the Toolbox .
13.  From the Toolbox, drag an InkCanvas control onto the WPF window .
14.  In the Properties window, set the following properties for the InkCanvas control .

| Property | Value |
| Width | Automatically |
| Height | Automatically |
| HorizontalAlignment | Stretching |
| VerticalAlignment | Stretching |
| Margin | 9, 9, 9, 68 |

15.  Change the color of the InkCanvas to yellow by setting its Background property to LightYellow .

The background of the InkCanvas control will be light yellow at run time.

16.  Drag two Button controls into the WPF window below the InkCanvas control . Position button1 on the left and button2 on the right.

17.  Select button1 and change the XAML markup in XAML view as shown in the following markup. This markup sets the text to Clear .

| <Button Height = "23" HorizontalAlignment = "Left" Margin = "85,0,0,24 "    Name = "button1" VerticalAlignment = "Bottom"    Width = "75"> Clear </Button> |

18.  Select button2 and change the XAML markup as shown in the following markup. This markup sets the text to Close .

| <Button Height = "23" HorizontalAlignment = "Right" Margin = "0,0,72,24"    Name = "button2" VerticalAlignment = "Bottom" Width = "75"    Click = "ButtonCloseClicked"> Close </Button> |

19.  The WPF application should resemble the Ink Pad application in the following illustration.

17.  Select button1 and change the XAML markup in view mode XAML , as shown below. This markup sets the text to Clear .

<Button Height = "23" HorizontalAlignment = "Left" Margin = "85,0,0,24" Name = "button1" VerticalAlignment = "Bottom" Width = " 75"> Clear </Button>

18.  Select button2 and change the XAML markup as shown below. This markup sets the text to Close .

<Button Height = "23" HorizontalAlignment = "Right" Margin = "0,0,72,24" Name = "button2" VerticalAlignment = "Bottom" Width = "75"

    Click = "ButtonCloseClicked"> Close </Button>

19.  The WPF application should resemble the Ink Pad application in the following figure .

WPF Ink Application
![WPF Ink Application](Image00020.jpg)  

To create event handlers

1.  Double-click Clear , and then add the following code to the generated Click event handler :

|     this.inkCanvas1.Strokes.Clear (); |

2.  Return to Designer view by right-clicking the Code Editor and then clicking Designer .
3.  Double-click Close , and then add the following code to the generated Click event handler:

|     this.Close (); |

4.  Press F5 to run the project.
5.  When the application opens, draw a picture in the InkCanvas control. If you make a mistake, you can click Clear to start over.
6.  Click Close to the close the application.

Creating event handlers

1.  Double click Clear and add the following code to the generated Click event handler .    

this.inkCanvas1.Strokes.Clear ();

2.  Return to Design view by right-clicking the code editor and choosing Design .
3.  Double click Close and add the following code to the generated Click event handler .    

this.Close ();

4.  Press the F5 key to execute the project.
5.  In the application that opens, draw a picture in the InkCanvas control . If you make a mistake, you can click the Clear button to start over.
6.  Click the Close button to close the application.

Designing a User Interface in Visual C #

In Visual C #, the most rapid and convenient way to create your user interface (UI) is to use the Windows Forms De signer and Toolbox .

There are three basic steps in creating all user interfaces:

*   Adding controls to the design surface.
*   Setting initial properties for the controls.
*   Writing handlers for specified events.

Although you can also create your UI by writing your own code, designers enable you to do this work much faster than is possible by manual coding.

Adding Controls

In the designer, you use the mouse to drag controls, such as buttons and text boxes, onto a design surface that represents the form. The followi ng illustration shows a combo box that has been dragged from the Toolbox onto a form in the Windows Forms Designer .

Creating a user interface in Visual C #

You can quickly and easily create user interfaces in Visual C # for Windows applications with the Windows Forms Designer and Toolbox .

There are three main steps in creating user interfaces for Windows Forms applications:

*   Adding controls to the design surface.
*   Setting initial properties for controls.
*   Writing handlers for given events.

Although you can create a user interface by writing your own code, using constructors can be much faster.

![clear](Image00021.jpg) Adding controls

Controls such as buttons and text boxes can be dragged onto the design surface that represents the form. The image below shows a combo box that was dragged from the Toolbox to a form in the Windows Fo rms designer .

![Toolbox](Image00022.jpg)

As you work visually, the designer translates your actions into C # source code and writes them into a project file that is named <name> .designer.cs where <name> is the name that you gave to the form. When your application runs, that source code will position and size your UI elements so that they appear just as they do on the design surface.

![Toolbox](Image00022.jpg)

When working in visual mode, the designer translates the actions you take into C # source code and writes them to a project file named name .designer.cs, where name is the name assigned to the form. When the application is executed, the source code will position the UI elements and resize them to match how they appear on the design surface.

![clear](Image00021.jpg) Setting Properties

After you add a control to your form, you can use the Properties window to set its properties, such as background color and default text. The values ​ ​ that you specify in the Properties window are the initial values ​ ​ that will be assigned to that property when the control is created at run time. In many cases, those values ​ ​ can be accessed or changed programmatically at run time by getting or setting the property on the instance of the control class in your application. The Properties window is useful at design time because it enables you to browse all the properties, events, and methods supported on a control. For more information, see Properties Window.

Handling Events

Programs with graphical user interfaces are primarily event-driven. They wait until a user does something such as entering text into a text box, clicking a button, or changing a selection in a list box. When that occurs, the control, which is just an instance of a .NET Framework class, sends an event to your application. You have the option of handling an event by writing a special method in your application that will be called when the event is received.

You can use the Properties window to specify which events you want to handle in your code. Select a control in the designer and click the Events button, with the lightning bolt icon, on the Properties window toolbar to see its events. The following diagram shows the events button.

![Events Button in the Properties Window](Image00023.jpg)

Setting properties

After you add a control to a form in the Properties window, you can set its properties, such as the background color and default text. The values ​ ​ set in the Properties window are the initial values ​ ​ that will be assigned to the corresponding property when the control is created at run time. In many cases, values ​ ​ can be accessed and changed programmatically at runtime by getting or setting properties on an instance of the control class in the application. The Property window can be useful at run time because it allows you to view all the properties, events, and methods supported by the control.

![clear](Image00021.jpg) Event handling

GUI programs are mostly event-based. Such programs wait for user action, such as entering text in a text box, pressing a button, or changing a selection in a combo box. When an action is executed, the control, which is just an instance of the .NET Framework class, dispatches an event to the application. To handle an event, you can write a special method in your application that will be called when the event is received.

In the Properties window, you can specify the events to be handled in your code. To view the events of a control, select it in the designer and click the Events with a lightning bolt icon in the Properties window . The following diagram displays an event button.

![Event button in the "Properties window"](Image00023.jpg)

When you add an event handler through the Properties window, the designer will automatically write the empty method body for you, and you must write the code to make the method do something useful. Most controls generate many events, but most of the time an application will only have to handle several of them, or even only one. For example, you probably have to handle a button's Click event, but you do not have to handle its Paint event unless you want to customize its appearance in some advanced way. Each control has a default event handler. You can create the default event handler by double-clicking the control in the designer. This creates the event handler and opens the Code Editor so that you can write code to handle the event.

How to: Use Button Controls

Buttons enable users to interact with your program. For example, many dialog boxes have an OK button and a Cancel button. Users can click the OK button to submit the information entered in the dialog box. Alternatively, they can click Cancel to close the dialog box without submitting any data.

You can set properties of a button to change its appearance. For example, you can set the Text property to display specific text on a button, or set the ForeColor property to change the color of the text.

Controls have events that are raised whenever a user performs a specific action on the control. You can create event handlers that determine how the program should respond to the event. All controls have a default event handler, and for a button, it is the Click event. The code that you write in the Click event handler of the button r uns whenever the user clicks the button.

When you add an event handler using the Properties window, the constructor will automatically write the main body of the empty method, and the user must write the code based on which the method will perform the necessary actions. Most controls generate many events, but an application will often need to handle only a fraction of them, or even just one. For example, you might want to handle the Click event for a button, but not its Paint event , unless you want to change its appearance in some additional way. Each control has a default event handler. To create a default event handler, double-click the control on the form. An event handler is created and a code editor opens to write an event to handle the event.

Using the Button Control

Buttons allow users to interact with the program. For example, many dialog boxes have OK and Cancel buttons. Users can click OK to submit the information entered in the dialog box. Otherwise, they can click Cancel to close the dialog without submitting data.

You can set properties to change its appearance. For example, you can set the Text property to display specific text on a button, or the ForeColor property to change the color of the text.

For controls, there are events that are raised when the user performs certain actions on the control. Creating event handlers allows you to define how your program should respond to an event. All controls have a default event handler; for a button, this handler is Click. The code written in the Click event handler is executed when the user clicks the button.

To use buttons in a program

1.  On the File menu, click NewProject .
2.  In the New Project dialog box, in the Templates pane, click Windows Forms Application .
3.  In the Name bo x, type ButtonExample , and then click OK .

A new Windows Forms project opens.

4.  From the Toolbox , drag a Button onto the form.
5.  In the Properties window, change the Text property to read: Display Date and then press ENTER .
6.  In the Properties window, click the drop-down arrow to the right of the ForeColor property, and then click the Custom tab of the dialog box that opens.
7.  Click the red box to apply red font to the text of the button.
8.  In the form, double-click the button to open the Code Editor.

The Code Editor opens in the middle of a method named button1_Click . This is the Click event handler. The code you write here will execute when the button is clicked.

9.  In the button1_Click event handler, type the following line of code.

| MessageBox.Show ( "Today is" +    DateTime.Today.ToLongDateString ()); |

10.  Press F5 to run your program.
11.  The program starts and the form appears. When you click the Button, a message box displays today's date.

How to: Call a Button's Click Event Programmatically

Even if a user does not click a button, you can raise th e button's Click event programmatically by using the PerformClick method. The following example demonstrates how to call the click event of a button within a program. When button2 is clicked, the click event for button1 is also triggered.

To use buttons in a program

1.  On the File menu, click NewProject .

| MessageBox.Show ( "button1.Click was raised." ); |

6.  Right-click the code, and then click View Designer .
7.  Double-click the second button ( button2 ) to create the Click event handler.
8.  In the button2_Click event handler, type the following line of code.

| // Call the Click event of button1.t est .PerformClick (); |

9.  Press F5 to run the program.
10.  The program starts and the form appears. When you click either button1 or button2 , the click event handler of button1 displays a message.

How to: Create a Group of Radio Buttons from a String Array

This example programmatically creates a group of Windows Forms RadioButton controls and set s their Text properties to values ​ ​ from an array of strings.

Compiling the Code

This example requires:

*   A Windows Form with a Button control named button1 . Set the Click event handler of button1 to button1_Click .

Example

| private void button1_Click (object sender, System.EventArgs e){    string [] stringArray = new string [3];    stringArray [0] = "Yes" ;    stringArray [1] = "No" ;    stringArray [2] = "Maybe" ;    System.Windows.Forms.RadioButton [] radioButtons =        new System.Windows.Forms.RadioButton [3];    for (int i = 0; i <3; ++ i)    {        radioButtons [i] = new RadioButton ();        radioButtons [i] .Text = stringArray [i];        radioButtons [i] .Location = new System.Drawing.Point (10, 10 + i * 20);        this .Controls.Add (radioButtons [i]);    }} |

How to: Create a Non-Rectangular Button

This example demonstrates how to cre ate a button that is a different shape from the standard rectangular button. The code adds a button in the shape of a circle to the form and creates an event handler that displays a message when the circle is clicked.

Compiling the Code

This example requires a Windows Forms Application project that contains a form named Form2.

Example

public Form2 ()

{

    //

    // Required for Windows Form Designer support.

    //

    InitializeComponent ();

    // Initialize the user-defined button,

    // including defining handler for Click message,

    // location and size.

    myButtonObject myButton = new myButtonObject ();

    EventHandler myHandler =

new EventHandler (myButton_Click);

    myButton.Click + = myHandler;

    myButton.Location = new System.Drawing.Point (20, 20);

    myButton.Size = new System.Drawing.Size (101, 101);

    this .Controls.Add (myButton);

}

public class myButtonObject: UserControl

{

    // Draw the new button.

    protected override void OnPaint (PaintEventArgs e)

    {

        Graphics graphics = e.Graphics;

        Pen myPen = new Pen (Color.Black);

        // Draw the button in the form of a circle

        graphics.DrawEllipse (myPen, 0, 0, 100, 100);

        myPen.Dispose ();

    }

}

// Handler for the click message.

void myButton_Click (Object sender, System.EventArgs e)

{

    MessageBox.Show ( "Click" );

}

How to: Display Text on a Windows Form

You can display text on a variety of controls, but the primary control for displaying text in you r program is the Label control. When you add a label to a form, the background appears in the same color as the form so that only the text is visible. You can also change the BackColor property of the label.

You can display text in the label by setting its Text property. The Font property determines the display font for the text in the Text property. The ForeColor property determines the color of the text itself.

To display text in a label

1.  On the File menu, click NewProject .
2.  In the New Project dialog box, click Windows Forms Application , and then click OK .

A new Windows Forms project opens.

3.  From the Toolbox , drag a Label onto the form.
4.  Add a Button control to the form, and change the following properties:

| Property | Value |
| Name | changeText |
| Text | Change text |
| Size | 80, 23 |

5.  Double-click the button to create the changeText_Click event handler, and add the following code:

| this .label1.Text = "Time" + DateTime.Now.ToLongTimeString (); |

Displaying Text on a Windows Form

You can display text in a wide variety of controls, but the main element in the program in which the text is displayed is the Label. When you add an inscription to a form, the background color matches the color of the form so that only the text is visible. You can change the BackColor property of the label.

To display text in a label, the Text property must be set to a specific value. The Font property defines the font for displaying the text contained in the Text property. The ForeColor property directly determines the color of the text.

Display text in a label

1.  On the File menu, choose New Project .
2.  In the New Project dialog box , select Windows Forms Application , and then click OK .

A new Windows Forms project will open.

3.  From the Toolbox, drag a Label control onto the form.
4.  Add a Button control to the form and change the following properties.

| Property | Value |
| Name | changeText |
| Text | Change text |
| The size | 80, 23 |

5.  Double click the button to create the changeText_Click event handler and add the following code.

| this .label1.Text = "Time" + DateTime.Now.ToLongTimeString (); |

6.  Add another Button control to the form, and change the following properties

| Property | Value |
| Name | changeColor |
| Text | Change color |
| Size | 80, 23 |

7.  Double-click the button to create the changeColor_Click event handler, and add the following code:

| Random randomColor = new Random ();this .label1.ForeColor = Color.FromArgb (randomColor.Next (0, 256),    randomColor.Next (0, 256), randomColor.Next (0, 256)); |

8.  Press F5 to run the program.
9.  Click Change Text and verify that the text in the label is updated.
10.  Click Change Color and verify that the font of the text is changed to a new color.

How to: Use TextBox Controls to Get User Input

You can both display text and retrieve text from users by using a TextBox control. After a user types data in a TextBox, you can retrieve this data by using the Text property. By default, the Multiline property of the TextBox is set to false . This means that users cannot press the ENTER key to create multiple lines of text in the TextBox. You can set the Multiline property to true to enable this.

6.  Add another Button control to the form and change the following properties.

| Property | Value |
| Name | changeColor |
| Text | Change color |
| The size | 80, 23 |

| Random randomColor = new Random ();this .label1.ForeColor = Color.FromArgb (randomColor.Next (0, 256),    randomColor.Next (0, 256), randomColor.Next (0, 256)); |

8.  Press the F5 key to execute the program.
9.  Click the Change Text button and make sure the text in the text box is updated.
10.  Click the Change color button and verify that the font color of the text has changed.

Using the TextBox Control to Receive Input

With the TextBox control, you can both display text and receive it from the user. User-entered data in the TextBox can be retrieved using the Text property . By default, the Multiline TextBox property is false . This means that the user does not can press the ENTER key to create I a few lines of text in the TextBox . To enable this feature, set the Multiline property to true .

| Property | Value |

| Text | Retrieve |

How to: Convert the Text in a TextBox Control to an Integer

When you provide a TextBox control in your application to retrieve a numeric value, you often have to convert the text (a string) to a numeric value, such as an integer. This example dem onstrates two methods of converting text data to integer data.

Example

| int anInteger;anInteger = Convert.ToInt32 (textBox1.Text);anInteger = int.Parse (textBox1.Text); |

Compiling the Code

Robust programming

The following conditions may cause an exception:

*   The text converts to a number that is too large or too small to store as an int .
*   The text may not represent a number.

How to: Load Text into a RichTextBox Control

This example loads a text file that a user selects in the OpenFileDialog. The code then populates a RichTextBox control with the file's contents.

Example

// Create an OpenFileDialog object.

OpenFileDialog openFile1 = new OpenFileDialog ();

// Initialize the filter to look for text files.

openFile1.Filter = "Text Files | * .txt" ;

// If the user selected a file, load its contents into the RichTextBox.

if (openFile1.ShowDialog () == System.Windows.Forms.DialogResult.OK)

    richTextBox1.LoadFile (openFile1.FileName, RichTextBoxStreamType.PlainText);

Dialog Boxes

How t o: Retrieve Data from a Dialog Box

Dialog boxes enable your program to interact with users and retrieve data that users have entered in the dialog box for use in your application. There are several built-in dialog boxes that you can use in your applications. You can also create your own dialog boxes.

A common task in Windows Forms development is displaying a dialog box to collect information when a user clicks a button on the main form of an application. You can see an example of this in the Visual C # Express Edition IDE. When you add a ListBox control to a form, you can click the Items property in the Properties window to display a dialog box. This dialog box enables you to quickly type text on separate lines of a text box. The application then adds each line of text to ListBox control when you click OK . If you want to let users add their own items to a list box in your application, you can create your own string editor dialog box. 

Dialog boxes

Retrieving Data from a Dialog Box

Dialog boxes allow the program to interact with users and retrieve the data they enter in the dialog box into the application. There are several built-in dialog boxes available for use in applications. You can also create your own dialog boxes.

A common task in Windows Forms development is to display a dialog box for collecting information after the user clicks a button in the main application form. The following example shows how to do this in Visual C # Express Edition of the IDE . After adding the ListBox control to the form, select the Items property in the Properties window to open the dialog box. With this dialog box, you can quickly enter text on multiple lines of a text box. After clicking OK, the application will add each line of text to the ListBox control . In order for users to add arbitrary elements to the list, you need to create your own string editor dialog box .

To create the main form of your application

1.  On the File menu, click New Project .

The New Project dialog box appears.

2.  Click Windows Forms Application , and then click OK .

A form named Form1 is added to the project.

Retrievin g Data from a Dialog Box

There are several ways you can pass data from one Windows form to another. In this example, you will pass Form1 to the constructor of Form2 .

To retrieve data from a dialog box.
