Standard WPF Controls

The following table shows the most common WPF controls, which you can find in the Common tab of the Toolbox .

| Control name | Description |
| System.Windows.Controls.Border | Di splays a border around content. |
| System.Windows.Controls.Button | Enables a user to perform an action by clicking a button. The Buttonbase.Click event occurs when a Button is clicked. |
| System.Windows.Controls.CheckBox | Enables a user to select and clear a check box to indicate a Yes / No or True / False value. |
| System.Windows.Controls.ComboBox | Enables a user to select an item from a drop-down list. The list is displayed when the user clicks a drop-down arrow. |
| System.Windows.Controls.Grid | Defines a flexible grid a rea that consists of columns and rows. |
| System.Windows.Controls.Image | Displays an image. |
| System.Windows.Controls.Label | Displays text on a form. Provides support for access keys. |

WPF Standard Controls

The following table lists the most common WPF controls that can be found on the Standard tab of the Toolbox .

| Control name | Description |
| System.Windows.Controls.Border | Displays a frame around the content. |
| System.Windows.Controls.Button | Allows the user to perform an action by clicking a button. The Buttonbase.Click event occurs when the Button control is clicked . |
| System.Windows.Controls.CheckBox | Allows the user to check and uncheck a check box to select a value like "Yes / No" or "True / False". |
| System.Windows.Controls.ComboBox | Allows the user to select an item from a dropdown list. The list is displayed when you click the drop-down arrow. |
| System.Windows.Controls.Grid | Defines a flexible grid area made up of columns and rows . |
| System.Windows.Controls.Image | Outputs an image. |
| System.Windows.Controls.Label | Outputs text on a form. Provides support for access keys. |

| System.Windows.Controls.ListBox | Enables a user to select an item from a list. |
| System.Windows.Controls.R adioButton | Enables a user to choose from among mutually exclusive items. The selection of one radio button is mutually exclusive to any other radio button in the same container. |
| System.Windows.Controls.StackPanel | Enables you to stack child controls vertically or horizontally. |
| System.Windows.Control.TabControl | Enables visual content to be arranged in a tabular form. |
| System.Windows.Controls.TextBox | Displays unformatted text and enables users to enter text. |

Additional controls available in the Toolbox include the following:

*   Container controls, such as System.Windows.Controls.Canvas , System.Windows.Controls.DockPanel , and System.Windows.Controls.Frame . For more information, see WPF Container Controls Overview.
*   Menus and Toolbars, such as System.Windows.Controls.Menu , System.Windows.Controls.ToolBar , and System.Windows.Controls.Primitives.StatusBar .
*   Document controls, such as System.Windows.Controls.DocumentViewer and System.Windows.Controls.FlowDocumentPageViewer .

| System.Windows.Controls.ListBox | Allows the user to select an item from a list. |
| System.Windows.Controls.RadioButton | Allows the user to select one of the mutually exclusive items. Selecting one of the radio buttons excludes selecting any other in the same container. |
| System.Windows. Controls.StackPanel | Allows you to arrange child controls horizontally or vertically. |
| System.Windows.Control.TabControl | Allows you to arrange visual content in tabular form. |
| System.Windows.Controls.TextBox | Displays unformatted text and allows users to enter text. |

Additional controls available in the toolbox .

*   Hosting controls such as System.Windows.Controls.Canvas , System.Windows.Controls.DockPanel, and System.Windows.Controls .Frame . For more information, see Understanding WPF Container Controls.
*   Menus and toolbars, such as System.Windows.Controls.Menu , System.Windows.Controls.ToolBar, and System.Windows.Controls.Primitives.StatusBar .
*   Controls for a document, such as System.Windows.Controls.DocumentViewer and System.Windows.Controls.FlowDocumentPageViewer .

How to: Create Event Handlers for WPF Controls

You can add the default event handler for many controls by double-clicking the con trol in Design view. You can also create an event handler for controls in a Windows Presentation Foundation (WPF) application by adding an attribute to the control's XAML representation. This XAML markup defines the event and the name of the method that will handle the event. Then, you write the code for the method in the Code Editor.

To create an event handler for a button

1.  Create a WPF application by using Visual C # Express Edition. For more information, see How to: Create a New WPF Application Project.
2.  Drag a Button from the Toolbox to the WPF window, and then select the button.
3.  Double-click the button.

The Click event handler is created and the cursor is positioned in the event handler in the Code Editor.

4.  Add the following code to the event handler:

| MessageBox.Show ("Event handler was created by" + "double-clicking the button."); |

5.  Drag a second Button control from the Toolbox to the WPF design surface, and then select the button.
6.  Add an attribute named Click to the Button element in the XAML editor, and set its value to ButtonOKClicked . This is the name that you will give the event handler in code. For example, the attribute can be written as follows: Click = "ButtonOKClicked" .
7.  Right-click the designer and then click View Code .
8.  Add the following event handler to the Window1 class. This code displays a message whenever you click the button.

| private void ButtonOKClicked (object sender, RoutedEventArgs e){    MessageBox.Show ("Event handler was created manually.");} |

9.  Press F5 to run the program.
10.  When the wind ow appears, click a button.
11.  Verify that the correct text appears in a message box when you click each button, and then close the application.

Creating Event Handlers for WPF Controls

For many controls, you can add default event handlers. To do this, in design mode, double-click the desired control. Alternatively, you can create an event handler for controls in a Windows Presentation Foundation ( WPF ) application by adding an attribute to the XAML view of the control. The XAML markup defines the event and the name of the method that will handle the event. Then, in the code editor, you can write the code for the method.

Creating an event handler for the button

1.  Create a WPF Application using Visual C # Express Edition.
2.  Drag a Button control from the Toolbox to the WPF window, and then select the button.
3.  Double click the button.

The Click event handler is created , and the mouse pointer is in the event handler in the code editor.

4.  Add the following code to your event handler.

| MessageBox.Show ("Event handler was created by" + "double-clicking the button."); |

5.  Drag a second Button control from the Toolbox onto the WPF design surface, and then select that button.
6.  Add an attribute named Click to the Button element in the XAML editor and set it to ButtonOKClicked . This is the name that will be assigned to the event handler in your code. For example, an attribute could be written like this: Click = "ButtonOKClicked"
7.  Right-click the designer, and then click View Code .
8.  Add the following event handler to the Window1 class . This code displays a message each time the button is pressed.

| private void ButtonOKClicked (object sender, RoutedEventArgs e){    MessageBox.Show ("Event handler was created manually.");} |

9.  Press the F5 key to execute the program.
10.  When the window appears, click the button.
11.  Make sure that the correct text appears in the message box each time you click each button, then close the application .

Connecting to Data

From accessing data in SQL Server Compact 3.5, to writing queries that use LINQ and binding data to controls on a Windows Form, Visual C # Express Edition includes tools and language features that make working with data easy.

How to: Connect to a Database

Connecting to a data source is easy when you use Visual C # Express Edition. You can use visual tools to browse to a database and add a local copy to your project. When you install SQL Server Compact 3.5 with Visual C # Express Edition, you have access to a sample database named Northwind.sdf. You can connect to the Northwind database by using the Data Sources window.

To add a connection to the Northwind database

1.  On the File menu, click New Project .
2.  In the New Project dialog box, click Windows Application , and then click OK .

A new Windows Forms project opens.

3.  On the Data menu, click Add New Data Source .

The Data Source Configuration Wizard opens.

4.  Click Database and then click Next .
5.  Click New Connection .

The Add Connection dialog box opens.

6.  In the Add Connection dialog box, if the Data source is not Microsoft SQL Server Compact 3.5 (.NET Framework Data Provider for Microsoft SQL Server Compact 3.5) , click Change and select Microsoft SQL Server Compact 3.5 from the Change Data Source dialog box. Click OK .

Data connection

In the Visual the C #, Express Edition provides tools and language features that facilitate working with data, ranging from data access to the SQL Server the Compact 3.5 to writing queries that use the LINQ , and to bind data to controls in the form of the Windows Forms .

Database connection

When using Visual C # Express Edition, connecting to a data source is straightforward. You can use visual tools to navigate to the database and add a local copy to your project. When you install SQL Server Compact 3.5 with Visual C # Express Edition, the user accesses a sample database named Northwind . sdf . You can use the Data Sources window to connect to the Northwind database .

Adding a connection to the "Northwind" database

1.  On the File menu, choose New Project .
2.  In the New Project dialog box , select Windows Forms Application , and then click OK .

A new Windows Forms project will open.

3.  On the Data menu, select Add New Data Source .

The Data Source Configuration Wizard opens .

4.  Click Database and click Next .
5.  Select New Connection .

The Add Connection dialog box opens .

6.  If, in the Add Connection dialog box, the Data Source has a value other than Microsoft SQL Server Compact 3.5 (.NET Framework Data Provider for Microsoft SQL Server Compact 3.5) , click Modify and in the Change Data Source dialog box , select Microsoft SQL Server Compact 3.5 . Click OK .

7.  Click the Brows e button and locate the Northwind.sdf database.

The default location is drive : \ Program Files \ Microsoft SQL Server Compact Edition \ v 3.5 \ Samples.

8.  Click Test Connection to verify that the connection succeeded, and then click OK to close the dialog box.
9.  Click OK to close the dialog box, and then in the Data Source Configuration Wizard , click Next . If prompted to copy the data file to your project, click Yes .
10.  On the next page of the wizard, select the Yes, save the connection as check box, and then click Next .
11.  On the Choose Your Database Objects page, expand Tables .
12.  Select the check box for both Products and Order Details , and then click Finish .

A local database file is added to your project. Notice that a NorthwindDataSet object has been added to the Data Sources window.

13.  In Solution Explorer , select Northwind.sdf and make sure that the Copy to Output Directory property is set to Copy if newer .
14.  On the File menu, click Save All to save your project.

7.  Click Browse and locate the Northwind.mdf database.

By default, it is located in the folder drive : \ Program Files \ Microsoft SQL Server Compact Edition \ v 3.5 \ Samples. [[33]](https://translate.googleusercontent.com/translate_f#_ftn33)

8.  Click the Test Connection button to verify that the connection was successful, and then click OK to close the dialog box.
9.  Click OK to close the dialog box, and then in the Data Source Configuration Wizard, click Next . When prompted to copy the data file to the project, click Yes .
10.  On the next page of the wizard, select the Yes, save the connection as check box , and then click Next .
11.  On the Select Database Objects page , expand the Tables node .
12.  Select the check boxes for the Products and Order Details tables , and then click Finish .

The local database file has been added to the project. Notice that the NorthwindDataSet object appears in the Data Sources window .

13.  In Solution Explorer, select Northwind.sdf and make sure the Copy to Output Directory property is set to Copy if newer .
14.  From the File menu, select Save All to save the project.

How to: Display Related Data on a Form

Data in separate tables can be related. When related data is in two tables, the relationship is visible in the Data Sources window. For example, if you connect to th e sample Northwind database and expand the Products table, you will see that an Order Details table is displayed as part of the Products table. If you drag this Order Details table or one or more of its fields to a Windows Form, it is displayed as part of a relationship with the Products table. For example, if you scroll through the products in the Products table, the corresponding order information for each product is automatically displayed.

To display related data on a form

1.  On the File menu, click New Project .
2.  In the New Project dialog box, click Windows Application , and then click OK .

A new Windows Forms project opens.

3.  Add a connection to the sample database named Northwind.sdf as described in [How to: Connect to a Database](https://translate.googleusercontent.com/translate_f#_How_to__Connect) .
4.  On the Data menu, click Show Data Sources .

The Data Sources window opens.

5.  In the Data Sources window, expand NorthwindDataSet , and then expand Products .
6.  Click the drop-down arrow next to Product Name , and then click Label .
7.  Drag the Product Name field to the Windows Form.
8.  Drag the Order Details table (the one that is located in the Products table) to the form, and position it under the label.

A DataGridView control is added to the form.

9.  Press F5 to run the program.
10.  When the application opens, click the Move Next Item button on the ProductsBindingNavigator at the top of the form.
11.  Verify that the order detail information in the DataGridView control is updated whenever the product changes.

Displaying related data in a form

Data in individual tables can be linked. If there is related data in two tables, the relationship will be displayed in the Data Sources window . For example, if you connect to the Northwind sample database and expand the Products table, you notice that the Order Details table appears as part of the Products table . If you drag this Order Details table, or one or more of its fields, onto a Windows Form, it appears as part of a relationship to the Products table . For example, scrolling through the products in the Products table automatically displays the corresponding order information for each product.

Displaying related data in a form

1.  On the File menu, choose New Project .
2.  In the New Project dialog box , select Windows Forms Application , and then click OK .

A new Windows Forms project will open.

3.  Add a connection to a sample database named Northwind.sdf as described in [Connecting to a Database](https://translate.googleusercontent.com/translate_f#_%D0%9F%D0%BE%D0%B4%D0%BA%D0%BB%D1%8E%D1%87%D0%B5%D0%BD%D0%B8%D0%B5_%D0%BA_%D0%B1%D0%B0%D0%B7%D0%B5) .
4.  On the Data menu, choose Show Data Sources .

The Data Sources window opens .

5.  In the Data Sources window , expand the NorthwindDataSet node and then the Products node .
6.  Click the drop-down arrow next to Product Name and select Label .
7.  Drag the Product Name field onto the Windows Form.
8.  Drag the Order Details table (the one in the Products table ) onto the form, placing it below the caption.

The DataGridView control is added to the form.

9.  Press the F5 key to execute the program.
10.  In an open application, click the Go to Next Item button in the ProductsBindingNavigator bar at the top of the form.
11.  Make sure the order information in the DataGridView is updated every time the product changes.

Using LINQ i n C # Express

It is very common for applications to use data in SQL databases or XML documents. Traditionally, developers had to learn a primary programming language, such as C #, and a secondary language, such as SQL or XQuery. Language-Integrated Query (LINQ) brings query capabilities into the C # language itself. Now, instead of learning a separate query language, you can use your knowledge of C #, together with some additional keywords and concepts, to query SQL databases, ADO.NET datasets, XML documents, and any .NET collection class that implements the IEnumerable interface ... 

Advantages of Using LINQ

*   Familiar syntax for writing queries.
*   Compile-time checking for syntax errors and type safety.
*   Improved debugger support.
*   IntelliSense support.
*   Ability to work directly with XML elements instead of creating a container XML document, as required with W3C DOM.
*   In-memory XML document modification that is powerful, yet simpler to use than XPath or XQuery.
*   Powerful filtering, ordering, and grouping capabilities.
*   Cons istent model for working with data across various kinds of data sources and formats.

Using LINQ in C # Express

Applications often use data from SQL databases or XML documents. Traditionally, developers have had to learn both a primary programming language such as C # and an additional programming language such as SQL or XQuery. LINQ (Language-Integrated Query) provides the ability to query in the C # language itself. Now, instead of learning a separate query language, you can query SQL databases, ADO.NET datasets, XML documents, and any .NET Framework collection classes that implement IEnumerable using your knowledge of C # and a few additional keywords and basic concepts.

Benefits of using LINQ

*   Familiar syntax for writing queries
*   Checking for syntax errors and compiling type safety.
*   Improved debugger support
*   IntelliSense support
*   The ability to work directly with XML elements instead of creating an XML document container, which was required when using the W3C Document Object Model (DOM).
*   Modifying an XML document in memory, which is more efficient than simply using XPath or XQuery.
*   Effective filtering, sorting and grouping functions.
*   A consistent model for working with data sources of various types and formats.

C # Language Enhancements that Support LINQ

The following are some new language structures that are added to C # 3.0 to support LINQ:

| Language Structure | Description |
| query expressions | A declarative q uery syntax used to query data from any LINQ-enabled data source. |
| implicitly typed variables | A variable, specified with the modifier var, which enables the compiler to infer the type of the variable. |
| object initializers | Enables the initialization of objects without explicitly calling a constructor for the object. |
| anonymous types | Enables the compiler to create objects without requiring that you specify a named data type. The type name is only available to the compiler. |
| extension methods | Enables the e xtension of any existing type by associating static methods to the type. |
| lambda expressions | An inline expression or statement block that can be used wherever a delegate type is expected. |

C # language extensions to support LINQ

Several new language structures added in C # 3.0 to support LINQ.

| Language structure | Description |
| query expressions | Declarative query syntax is used to query data from any data source supported by LINQ. |
| implicitly typed variables | The var modifier in a variable declaration allows the compiler to determine its type. |
| object initializers | Allows you to initialize an object without explicitly calling a constructor. |
| anonymous types | Allows the compiler to create objects without specifying a named data type. The type name is only available to the compiler. |
| extension methods | Allows you to extend any existing type by associating it with static methods. |
| lambda expressions | A built-in expression or statement block that can be used whenever a delegate type is required. |

Writing LINQ Queries

Whether you are working with ADO.NET datasets, SQL databases, .NET collections, or XML documents, the basic structure of a LINQ query expression is the same. A query expression is starts with a from clause, followed by query clauses such as where , orderby , select , and so on. The complete expression is stored in a query variable which can be executed or modified any number of times. Query expression syntax resembles the syntax of SQL. For example, you could write a LINQ query that returns all students in a students database that have science as their major, by using the following syntax:

IEnumerable <Student> studentQuery =

from student in studentApp.students

where student.Major == "Science"

select student;

LINQ to Objects

The phrase, LINQ to Objects, refers to the use of LINQ to query in-memory data collections such as the classes in the System.Collections and System.Collections.Generic namespaces. These classes include ArrayList , List <T> , Dictionary <K, T> , and so on. You can query arrays because they implicitly support IEnumerable <T>.  

You can even import a text file into enumerable data structures and run queries to filter or sort its contents.

Writing LINQ Queries

The basic structure of a LINQ query expression remains unchanged whether the data source is ADO.NET dataset, SQL database, .NET Framework collections, or XML documents. A query expression begins with a from clause , followed by other query expressions such as where , orderby , select, and so on. The query variable stores the complete query expression, which can be executed or modified any number of times. Query expression syntax is similar to SQL syntax. For example, you can write a LINQ query that returns all the students in the base of students with specialization in science ™ , using the following syntax:

IEnumerable <Student> studentQuery =

from student in studentApp.student s

where student.Major == "Science "

select student ;

LINQ to Objects

The term LINQ to Objects refers to the use of LINQ to query collections of data in memory , such as the classes in the spaces of names System.Collections and the System.Collections.Generic . These classes include ArrayList , List <T> , Dictionary <K, T>, and so on . You can query arrays because they implicitly support the IEnumerable <T> generic interface.

You can even import a text file into an enumerated structure and run queries that filter and sort the content about.

LINQ to SQL

Use LINQ to SQL to access SQL Server and SQL Server Express databases through a strongly-typed object layer that you create by using the O / R Designer.

You can use the O / R Designer to map LINQ to SQL classes to tables in a database and then write LINQ queries to bind data to controls in your application. For example, the following LINQ query binds the results of a LINQ query (all customers from the United States) to a binding source of a DataGridView control.

| var CustomersQuery = from customers in northwindSampleDataContext1.Customers                      where customers.Country == "US"                      select customers;customerBindingSource.DataSource = CustomersQuery; |
| Note:The O / R Designer currently does not support SQL Server Compact 3.5 databases. |

LINQ to DataSet

The DataSet is used to bind data to controls in an application. Rather than connecting directly to the database, the DataSet enables an application to use off-line (cached) data, or subsets of several data sources. When the application is brought online, the changes in the DataSet can be updated in the database.

LINQ to Dataset makes querying over cached data faster and easier than the filtering and sorting methods available to a DataSet.

LINQ to XML

LINQ to XML enables you to create and modify XML documents easily by using LINQ query expressions instead of XPath or XQuery. LINQ to XML is a new in-memory XML programming API that uses modern programming constructs instead of the W3C Document Object Model (DOM).

LINQ to SQL

Use LINQ to SQL to access SQL Server and SQL Server Express databases through a strongly typed object layer that you create with the Object Relational Designer.

You can use the Object Relational Designer to map LINQ to SQL classes to tables in a database and then create LINQ queries to bind data to controls in your application. For example, the following LINQ query binds the results of a LINQ query (all US clients) to a binding source with a DataGridView control.

| var CustomersQuery = from customers in northwindSampleDataContext1.Customers                      where customers.Country == "US"                      select customers;customerBindingSource.DataSource = CustomersQuery; |
| Note.The OR Designer does not currently support SQL Server Compact 3.5 databases. |

LINQ to DataSet

DataSet is used to bind data to controls in the application. Instead of connecting directly to the database, you can use a DataSet to create a standalone data cache or a subset of multiple data sources. When the application is brought online, changes to the DataSet will be made in the database.

LINQ to Dataset queries cached data faster and easier than the filtering and sorting methods available on the DataSet.

LINQ to XML

LINQ to XML makes it easy to create and modify XML documents using LINQ query expressions instead of using XPath and XQuery. LINQ to XML is a new programming API for in-memory XML that uses modern programming constructs instead of the W3C Document Object Model (DOM).

C # Language Primer

Inside a C # Program

In order to understand how a C # program works, let's examine the traditional "Hello World!" program, dealing with each line of C # code in turn.

Hello World, C # Style

The C # language uses classes to organize and package code. In fact, all executable C # code must be contained in a class, even in a short program like "Hello World!" Here is the complete program that displays "Hello World!" in the console window.

| using System;// A "Hello World!" program in C #namespace HelloWorld{    class Hello    {        static void Main ()        {            System.Console.WriteLine ( "Hello World!" );        }    }} |

C # language for beginners

C # program structure

To understand how a C # program works, let's look at a traditional "Hello World!" and break down each line of its C # code.

![clear](Image00021.jpg) "Hello World" in C # style

Classes are used to streamline and format code in C #. In fact, all the executable C # code must be contained in a class, which is also true for a short program like "Hello World!" The following is the complete text of the program that displays the "Hello World!" Message in the console window.

using System;

// A Hello World! program in C #

namespace HelloWorld

{

    class Hello

    {

        static void Main ()

        {

            System.Console.WriteLine ("Hello World!");

            // Keep the console window open in debug mode.

            System.Console.WriteLine ("Press any key to exit.");

            System.Console.ReadKey ();

        }

    }

}

![clear](Image00021.jpg)

using Directives and Namespaces

When you create a console application with Visual C #, the first lines in the code editor contain using directives listing several .NET Framework namespaces. A namespace is a way of grouping classes and structs together in a way that limits their scope and avoids name conflicts with other classes and structs. When you create a program in Visual C #, a namespace is automatically created for you. To use classes from other namespaces in your program, you must specify them with a using Directive. The most commonly used .NET Framework namespaces are listed by default when you create a new application. If you use classes from other namespaces in the class library, you must add a using directive for that namespace to the source file.

When the Code Editor detects that you have declared a class or struct that it cannot find in the namespaces listed in the current using directives, it will suggest namespaces that contain the class or struct.

Comments

After the using statements, the next line contains a comment. Comments are useful for including notes to yourself or other programmers.

| // A "Hello World!" program in C # |

The characters // convert the rest of the line to a comment. You can also comment a block of text by placing it between the characters / * and * / , for example:

| / * A "Hello World!" program in C #.This program displays the string "Hello World!" on the screen. * / |

You can also use a formatting option to comment code automatically.

Directive using and space names

When you create a console application with Visual C #, the first line in the code editor contains a using directive listing several .NET Framework namespaces. The namespace allows, in a sense, to group together classes and structures, which limits their scope and avoids name conflicts with other classes and structures. When you create a program in Visual C #, the namespace is created automatically. To use classes from other namespaces in a program, you must specify them with the using directive . When you create a new application, the most commonly used .NET Framework namespaces are listed by default. When using classes from other namespaces in the class library, you must add the using directive for the namespace to the source file.

When the code editor determines that the declared class or structure does not appear in the namespaces in the current list of the using directive , it will suggest a namespace containing that class or structure.

![clear](Image00021.jpg) Notes

The line following the using statement contains a comment. Comments are useful for including notes for yourself or other programmers .

// A Hello World! program in C #

The // characters convert the rest of the line to a comment. You can also comment out a block of text by placing it between the characters / * and * / , as in the example .

/ * A "Hello World!" program in C #.

This program displays the string "Hello World!" on the screen. * /

You can also use formatting options to automatically comment out your code.

Classes

The C # language uses classes to package code: all executable C # code must be contained in a class.

Main ()

*   The C # program must contain a Main method, in which control starts and ends. The Main method is where you create objects and execute other methods. The Main method is a static method that resides inside a class or a struct. In the "Hello World!" example, it resides inside the Program class.

The Main method can be defined in one of the following ways:

*   It can return void:

| static void Main (){    // ...} |

*   It can also return an int:

| static int Main (){    // ...    return 0;} |

*   It can take arguments, which are useful for command line utilities:

| static void Main (string [] args){    // ...} |

*   -or-

| static int Main (string [] args){    // ...    return 0;} |

The parameter of the Main method is a string array that represents the command-line arguments used to invoke the program. Notice that unlike C ++, this array does not include the name of the executable (.exe) file.

![clear](Image00021.jpg) Classes

In C #, classes are used to style code: all executable C # code must be contained in a class

![clear](Image00021.jpg) Main ()

*   The C # program must have a Main method where control starts and ends. In the Main method , objects are created and other methods are executed. The Main method is a static method located inside a class or structure. In the previous example, "Hello World!" it is located in a class named Program .

The Main method can be declared in one of the following ways:

*   It returns [void](https://translate.google.com/translate?hl=ar&prev=_t&sl=ru&tl=en&u=http://msdn.microsoft.com/ru-ru/library/yah0tteb.aspx) .

static void Main ()

{

    // ...

}

*   It can also return an [int](https://translate.google.com/translate?hl=ar&prev=_t&sl=ru&tl=en&u=http://msdn.microsoft.com/ru-ru/library/5kzh1b5w.aspx) .

static int Main ()

{

    // ...

    return 0;

}

*   It can take arguments, which can be useful for command line programs.

static void Main (string [] args)

{

    // ...

}

or

static int Main (string [] args)

{

    // ...

    return 0;

}

The Main method parameter is an array of [string](https://translate.google.com/translate?hl=ar&prev=_t&sl=ru&tl=en&u=http://msdn.microsoft.com/ru-ru/library/362314fe.aspx) values representing the command line arguments used to invoke the program. Note that unlike C ++, the array does not contain the name of the executable (EXE) file.

Console Input and Output

C # console programs generally use the input / output services provided by .NET Framework Console class. The statement Console.WriteLine ("Hello, World!"); uses the WriteLine method. It displays its string parameter on the command-line window followed by a new line. Other Console methods are used for different input and output operations. The Console class is a member of the System namespace. If the using System; statement was not included at the beginning of the program, you would have to specify the System classes like this:

| System.Console.WriteLine ( "Hello World!" ); |

The WriteLine method is very useful, and you will use it a lot if you are writing console applications.

WriteLine can display strings:

| Console.WriteLine ( "Hello World!" ); |

WriteLine can also display numbers:

| int x = 42;Console.WriteLine (x); |

If you need to display several items, use {0} to represent the first item, {1} the second item, and so on, like this:

| int year = 1066;string battle = "Battle of Hastings" ;Console.WriteLine ( "The {0} took place in {1}." , Battle, year); |

The output will look like this:

The Battle of Hastings took place in 1066.

![clear](Image00021.jpg) Console input and output

C # console programs typically use the I / O services provided by the .NET Framework Console class . Statement Console.WriteLine ("Hello, World!"); uses the WriteLine method . It outputs its string parameter to the command prompt window on a new line. Other Console methods are used for various input and output operations. The Console class is a member of the System . If you put the directive at the beginning of the program [[34]](https://translate.googleusercontent.com/translate_f#_ftn34) using System; , then you will need to specify the System classes , as in the following example.

System.Console.WriteLine ( "Hello World!" );

The WriteLine method is very useful and is often used when building console applications.

The WriteLine method can display lines .

Console . WriteLine ( " Hello World !" );

The WriteLine method can also display numbers .

int x = 42;

Console.WriteLine (x);

If you need to display multiple items, use {0} to represent the first item, {1} for the second item, and so on, as follows .

int year = 1066;

string battle = "Battle of Hastings" ;

Console.WriteLine ( "The {0} took place in {1}." , Battle, year);

The result will look like this. [[35]](https://translate.googleusercontent.com/translate_f#_ftn35)

The Battle of Hastings took place in 1066.

Program Structure and Flow of Execution

When you create a C # application, you have the choice of creating a Console application or a Windows Forms application. The two differ not only in the type of user interface; they can also differ in their execution flow.

Windows Forms Applications

In a typical Windows-based application that has a graphical user interface, most of the action after initial startup occurs in response to user actions such as moving the mouse, selecting a menu option, or typing text. Such actions trigger events and special methods in your application named event handlers are called. Almost everything a Windows-based program does is initiated by an event handler. When no events are being generated, the program does nothing.

If you are used to procedural programming languages, such as COBOL, BASIC, or FORTRAN, you will have to get used to the event-driven model. The most fundamental difference is that, in event-driven programming, other software, and the operating system itself, is calling event-handler methods in your application. You do not know which methods they will call. You can decide which events to handle in your application, but you cannot know in advance the exact order in which those events will occur.

In a typical Windows-based application, fields, arrays, and collections that hold the application's state are put in the main Form class that is named Form1 by default. At the scope of the class, these members are accessible from all the event handler methods that are implemented in the same Form class. When an event handler is called, it may do something to modify the application data, and when the method returns, the application resumes its waiting state. For example, a form may contain a TextBox control and an Update button. When a user clicks the button, the application's event handler might get the text in the TextBox , for example, and then add it to a list of other strings that are stored at the scope of the class. After the string has been added, the application returns to the waiting state. Other event handlers may perform other kinds of actions on that same list of strings in response to user input.

Your own custom classes can send and receive events by using the same mechanisms as Windows Forms.

Program structure and progress

When you create a C # application, you need to choose its type: console application or Windows Forms application. In addition to differences in the type of user interface, they may also differ in the way they are executed.

![clear](Image00021.jpg) Windows Forms Applications

In a typical Windows GUI application, most of the initial startup activity occurs in response to user input, such as moving the mouse, selecting a menu item, or entering text. Such events cause triggers to fire and call special methods called event handlers . Almost everything that a Windows program does is triggered by an event handler. In the absence of events, the program is inactive.

Unlike procedural programming languages ​ ​ such as COBOL, BASIC, or FORTRAN, it uses an event-driven model. The most fundamental difference in event-driven programming is that other software and the operating system itself call event handler methods in the user's application. It is unknown what methods they will call. You can decide which events to handle in your application, but you cannot know the exact order in which they will occur.

In a typical Windows application, the fields, arrays, and collections that store application state information reside in the main Form class , which is named Form1 by default . In class scope, these members are available from all event handler methods implemented in the same Form class . When called, an event handler can change something in the application data, and when it finishes, the application will return to a pending state. For example, a form might contain a TextBox element and an Update button . When the user clicks the button , the application event handler can take the text from the TextBox and then, for example, add it to the list of other strings that are stored in the class scope. After adding the row, the application will return to the idle state. In response to user input, other event handlers can perform different kinds of actions on the same list of strings.

Custom classes can send and receive events using the same mechanisms as Windows Forms.

Console Applications

In many console applications, the flow of execution proceeds from one statement to the next until the end of the program is reached and the application terminates. Of course, this isn't always the case because a console application can still be driven by keyboard events and system events that are generated by objects such as timers and network connections. Simple console applications often consist of only one class, the one that contains the Main method. However, more complex applications may contain any number of classes.

Namespaces

Namespaces are a way of organizing the various types that occur in a C # program. It is somewhat similar in concept to a folder in a computer file system. Like folders, namespaces enable classes to have a unique fully-qualified name. AC # program contains one or more namespaces, and each namespace is either defined by you the programmer, or defined as part of a previously written class library.

For example, the namespace System includes the Console class, a class that contains methods for reading and writing to the console window. The System namespace also contains a number of other namespaces, such as System.IO and System.Collections. The .NET Framework alone has more than eighty namespaces, each with up to thousands of classes: namespaces are used to minimize the confusion that might be caused between similarly named types and methods.

If you write a class outside of a namespace declaration, the compiler will supply a default namespace for that class.

![clear](Image00021.jpg) Console Applications

In many console applications, execution proceeds from statement to statement until the end of the program is reached and the application exits. Of course, this is not always the case, because the console application can also be controlled from the keyboard and system events, which are generated by objects such as timers and network connections. A simple console application often consists of only one class, which contains the Main method . However, more complex applications can contain any number of classes.

Namespaces

Namespaces are a way of organizing the various types found in C # programs . They can be compared to a folder on a computer file system. Like folders, namespaces define unique fully qualified names for classes. A C # program contains one or more namespaces, each of which is either defined by the programmer or defined as part of a previously written class library.

For example, the System namespace contains the Console class , which includes methods for reading and writing to a console window. The System namespace includes a number of other spaces, such as System . IO and System . Collections . Only in the platform. NET Framework provides more than 80 namespaces, each with thousands of classes, as namespaces are used to avoid confusion with types and methods that have the same name.

When you write a class outside of a namespace declaration, the compiler will provide it with a default namespace.

Namespace Shortcuts

To use the method WriteLine, defined in the Console class contained in the System namespace, you would use a line of code like this:

| System.Console.WriteLine ( "Hello, World!" ); |

Remembering to precede all the methods contained in Console with System would quickly become tiresome, so a useful shortcut is to insert the using Directive at the start of your C # source file, like this:

| using System; |

Including using System; establishes that the System namespace is assumed, and you can subsequently write just this:

| Console.WriteLine ( "Hello, World!" ); |

Creating Your Own Namespace

It is common to use namespaces when working on large programs. Using your own namespaces provides a degree of control over similarly named methods and types. For example, assume you are writing an application that loads both statistical data and image files from a disk. You could create two new namespaces, one called Images and one called StatisticalData . As you are using two separate namespaces, all the names of the methods defined in each namespace will be unique, even if the individual classes have the same name. This means you could have a class called FileHandling in both namespaces, both containing a method called Load . You could specify the class you wanted by referring to either StatisticalData.FileHandling or Images.FileHandling .

It is good practice to create a separate folder for each namespace in your Visual C # Express project.

![clear](Image00021.jpg) Accelerated Namespace Methods

To use the WriteLine method defined in the Console class that is contained in the System namespace , use a line of code as shown below.

System.Console.WriteLine ( "Hello, World!" );

Remembering that all the methods contained in the Console must be preceded by System quickly becomes tedious, so it is a good idea to insert a using directive at the top of your C # source file , as shown below .

using System;

Adding using System; establishes that the System namespace is assumed and can subsequently write the following .

Console . WriteLine ( " Hello , World !" );

![clear](Image00021.jpg) Creating your own namespace

Typically namespaces are used when working with large programs. Native namespaces provide some level of control over methods and types with the same name. For example, suppose you are writing an application that loads statistics and image files from disk. You can create two new namespaces, one named Images and one named StatisticalData . Since there are two separate spaces, all method names defined in each will be unique, even if the individual classes have the same name. Suppose that in both spaces is the class name FileHandling and each class has a method called the Load . You can refer to StatisticalData.FileHandling or Images.FileHandling to specify the desired class .

It is recommended to create a separate folder for each namespace in a Visual C # project.

Example

The following example defines two namespaces, each containing a class named FileHandling . By specifying the namespace, it's possible to quickly differentiate between the classes and the methods they contain.

| namespace StatisticalData{    class FileHandling    {        public void Load () {}  // code to load statistical data    }}namespace Images{    class FileHandling    {        public void Load () {}  // code to load an image file    }}class Program{    static void Main ()    {        StatisticalData.FileHandling data = new StatisticalData.FileHandling ();        data.Load ();        Images.FileHandling image = new Images.FileHandling ();        image.Load ();    }} |

Example

The following example defines two namespaces, each containing a class named FileHandling . By specifying a namespace , you can quickly distinguish classes from methods .

 ------

Classes

C # is an object-oriented programming language, and in common with other modern languages, it groups related fields, methods, properties, and events into data structures called classes.

![clear](Image00021.jpg) Classes vs. Objects

A class is basically a blueprint for a custom data type. Once you define a class, you use it by loading it into memory. A class that has been loaded into memory is called an object or an instance . You create an instance of a class by using the C # keyword new.

Here is an example of a class definition called SampleClass , and the creation of an object called sampleClass1 that is an instance of that class. Because C # requires that the Main function be defined inside a class, the following code also defines a Program class, but that class is not used to create an object.

| using System;class SampleClass{    public void SayHello ()    {        Console.WriteLine ( "Hello, World!" );    }}class Program{    // Main is the entrypoint, where every C # program starts    static void Main (string [] args)    {        SampleClass sampleClass1 = new SampleClass ();  // Create an object        sampleClass1.SayHello ();                       // Call a method    }} |

Just as you can build any number of houses based on the same blueprint, you can instantiate any number of objects of the same class. It is very common to have arrays or lists that contain many objects of the same class. Each instance of the class occupies a separate memory space and the values ​ ​ of its fields (except its static fields as discussed below) are separate and independent. In the code example below, you could create one object of type Animal and set its size to 2, and another object whose size you set to 3\. However, there is an important exception to this rule, and that is the static member.

![clear](Image00021.jpg) Classes

C # is an object-oriented programming language and, like other modern languages, groups related fields, methods, properties, and events into data structures called classes.

Classes and objects

A class is essentially a blueprint for a custom data type. Once the class is defined, it can be used by loading it into memory. A class loaded into memory is called an object or instance . The class is instantiated using the C # new keyword.

The following is an example of defining a class named SampleClass and creating an object named sampleClass1 that is an instance of that class. Because the Main function needs to be defined within the class, the following code also defines the Program class , but it is not used to create the object .

using System;

class SampleClass

{

    public void SayHello ()

    {

        Console.WriteLine ( "Hello, World!" );

    }

}

class Program

{

    // Main is the entrypoint, where every C # program starts

    static void Main ( string [] args)

    {

        SampleClass sampleClass1 = new SampleClass (); // Create an object

        sampleClass1.SayHello (); // Call a method

    }

}

Just as you can build several buildings from one drawing, you can create any number of objects of the same class. Arrays or lists containing many objects of the same class are often used. Each instance of the class occupies a separate area of ​ ​ memory; the values ​ ​ of its fields (excluding static fields, as described below) are also independent. The following code example creates one object of type Animal , sets it to size "2", and another object to size "3". However, there is an important exception to this rule, namely the static member .

Static vs. Instance Members

A static member is a method or field that can be accessed without reference to a particular instance of a class. The most common static method is Main , which is the entry point for all C # programs; note that you do not need to create an instance of the containing class in order to call the Main method. Another commonly used static method is WriteLine in the Console class. Note the difference in syntax when accessing static methods; you use the class name, not the instance name, on the left side of the dot operator: Console.WriteLine .

When you declare a class field static, all instances of that class will "share" that field. If size in the following code example were declared static, and one Animal object changed the value, the value would be changed for all objects of type Animal .

A static class is one whose members are all static. Static classes, methods and fields are useful in certain scenarios for performance and efficiency reasons. However, subtle errors can arise if you assume that a field is an instance field when in fact it is static. For more information, see Static Classes and Static Class Members (C # Programming Guide).

Classes vs. Files

Every C # program has at least one class. When designing your program, it is good practice, but not a requirement, to keep a single class in each source code (.cs) file. If you use the C # integrated development environment (IDE) to create classes, it will automatically create a new source code file for you at the same time.

![clear](Image00021.jpg) Instance Members and Static Members

A static member is a method or field that can be accessed without reference to a specific instance of the class. The most common static method is Main , which represents the entry point for all C # programs; Note that you do not need to instantiate the containing class to call Main . Another commonly used static method is WriteLine in the Console class . When accessing static methods, note the difference in syntax; on the left side of the dot (period) statement , the class name is used instead of the instance name: Console.WriteLine .

A class field declared as static will be shared across all instances of the class. If size in the following code example were declared static and one Animal object has changed the value, the value will change for all Animal objects .

In a static class, all elements are also static. The use of static classes, methods and fields is advisable in a number of cases to improve performance and efficiency. However, if a field is assumed to be an instance field when it is actually static, implicit errors can occur.

![clear](Image00021.jpg) Classes and files

Every C # program has at least one class. When developing a program, it is recommended (but not required) to keep a separate class in each source code file (CS file). If you use the C # IDE to create your classes, it will automatically generate a source code file at the same time.

Encapsulation

A class typically represents the characteristics of a thing, and the actions that it can perform. For example, to represent an animal as a C # class, you might give it a size, speed, and strength represented as numbers, and some functions such as MoveLeft () , MoveRight () , SpeedUp () , Stop () and so on , in which you would write the code to make your "animal" perform those actions. In C #, your animal class would look like this:

|     public class Animal    {        private int size;        private float speed;        private int strength;                  public void MoveLeft ()  // method                  {                                // code goes here ...        }        // other methods go here ...    } |

If you browse through the .NET Framework Class Library , you will see that each class represents a "thing," such as an XmlDocument, a String, a Form, and that each thing has various actions that it can perform (methods), characteristics that you can read and perhaps modify (properties) and notifications (events) that it sends out when it performs some given action. The methods, properties and events, along with all the other internal variables and constants (fields), are referred to as the members of the class.

Grouping members together into classes is not only logical, it also enables you to hide data and functions that you do not want other code to have access to. This principle is known as encapsulation . When you browse the .NET Framework class libraries, you are seeing only the public members of those classes. Each class probably also has private members that are used internally by that class or classes related to it, but are not meant to be used by applications. When you create your own classes, you will decide which members should be public, and which should be private.

![clear](Image00021.jpg) Encapsulation

Typically, a class represents the characteristics of an object and the actions it performs. For example, to represent an animal as a C # class, you need to give it size, speed and strength, represented as numbers, as well as some functions, such as MoveLeft () , MoveRight () , SpeedUp () , Stop (), and so on, in which you can write code to do these things for the animals. In C #, an animal class might look like this .

    public class Animal

    {

        private int size;

        private float speed;

        private int strength;

        public void MoveLeft () // method

        {

            // code goes here ...

        }

        // other methods go here ...

    }

When looking at the .NET Framework Class Library it will be clear that each class represents an "object" like XmlDocument , String , Form , and for each such "object" there are different actions that it can perform (methods), characteristics that can be read and modify (properties) and notifications (events) sent when some specified actions are performed. Methods, properties and events, as well as all other internal variables and constants (fields) are called class members.

Grouping members into classes not only makes logical sense, it also allows you to hide data and functions that should not be available to other code. This principle is called encapsulation . When you browse the .NET Framework class libraries, you will only see the public members of those classes. It is possible that each class has private members that are used internally by that class or related classes that are not intended to be used by applications. When you create your own classes, you need to decide which members are public and which are private.

Inheritance

A class can inherit another class, which means that it includes all the members, public and private, of the original class, plus the additional members that it defines. The original class is called the base class and the new class is called the derived class . You create a derived class to represent something that is a more specialized kind of the base class. For example, you could define a class Cat that inherits from Animal . A Cat can do everything an Animal can do, plus it can perform one additional unique action. The C # code looks like this:

|     public class Cat: Animal    {                  public void Purr ()                  {                  }    } |

The Cat: Animal notation indicates that Cat inherits from Animal and that therefore Cat also has a MoveLeft method and the three private variables size , speed , and strength . If you define a SiameseCat class that inherits from Cat , it will have all the members of Cat plus all the members of Animal .

Polymorphism

In the field of computer programming, polymorphism refers to the ability of a derived class to redefine, or override , methods that it inherits from a base class. You do this when you need to do something specific in a method that is either different or else not defined in the base class. For example, the Animal.MoveLeft method, since it must be very general in order to be valid for all animals, is probably very simple: something like "change orientation so that animal's head is now pointing in direction X". However, in your Cat class, this might not be sufficient. You might need to specify how the Cat moves its paws and tail while it turns. And if you have also defined a Fish class or a Bird class, you will probably need to override the MoveLeft method in different ways for each of those classes also. Because you can customize the behavior of the MoveLeft method for your particular class, the code that creates your class and calls its methods does not have a separate method for each animal in the world. As long as the object inherits from Amimal , the calling code can just call the MoveLeft method and the object's own version of the method will be invoked.

![clear](Image00021.jpg) Inheritance

A class can inherit from another class, which means that it includes all of the members — public and private — of the original class, as well as additional members that it defines. The original class is called the base class and the new class is called the derived class . A derived class is created to represent the special capabilities of a base class. For example, you can define a class called Cat that inherits from Animal . Cat can do everything that Animal can do, plus one unique action. The C # code looks like this .

    public class Cat: Animal

    {

        public void Purr ()

        {

        }

    }

Notation Cat: Animal means , that Cat inherits from Animal , and that Cat also has a method MoveLeft and three closed variables : size bed , speed and strength . If you define a SiameseCat class that inherits from Cat , it will contain all Cat members as well as all Animal members .

![clear](Image00021.jpg) Polymorphism

In computer programming, polymorphism refers to the ability of a derived class to modify or override the methods that it inherits from the base class. This function is used when a method that differs or is not defined in the base class needs to perform some special action. For example, since the Animal.MoveLeft method needs to be generic to be suitable for all animals, it is perhaps very simple, such as "changing position so that the animal's head is in the X direction". However, for a Cat class, this may not be enough. You may need to specify how the Cat moves its legs and tail when cornering. And if the Fish class or the Bird class is already defined, you may need to override the MoveLeft method in different ways for each of the classes. Because you can customize the behavior of the MoveLeft method for a specific class, the code that creates the class and calls its methods does not have a separate method for each animal. As long as an object inherits from A n imal , callers can only call MoveLeft and its own version of the object's method.

![clear](Image00021.jpg) Constructors

Every class has a constructor: a method that shares the same name as the class. The constructor is called when an object is created based on the class definition. Constructors typically set the initial values ​ ​ of variables defined in the class. This is not necessary if the initial values ​ ​ are to be zero for numeric data types, false for Boolean data types, or null for reference types, as these data types are initialized automatically.

You can define constructors with any number of parameters. Constructors that do not have parameters are called default constructors . The following example defines a class with a default constructor and a constructor that takes a string, and then uses each:

| class SampleClass{    string greeting;    public SampleClass ()    {        greeting = "Hello, World" ;    }    public SampleClass (string message)    {        greeting = message;    }    public void SayHello ()    {        System.Console.WriteLine (greeting);    }}class Program{    static void Main (string [] args)    {        // Use default constructor.        SampleClass sampleClass1 = new SampleClass ();        sampleClass1.SayHello ();        // Use constructor that takes a string parameter.        SampleClass sampleClass2 = new SampleClass ( "Hello, Mars" );        sampleClass2.SayHello ();    }} |

Constructors

Each class has a constructor - a method with the same name as the class. The constructor is called when an object is created based on the class definition. Typically, constructors set initial values ​ ​ for variables defined in a class. Constructors are not used if the initial value for numeric data types is zero, "false" for boolean data types, or null for reference types, since these data types are automatically initialized.

You can define constructors with any number of parameters. Parameterless constructors are called default constructors . The following example shows a class definition with a default constructor and a constructor that accepts a string expression. Then this class uses each one .

class SampleClass

{

    string greeting ;

    public SampleClass ()

    {

        greeting = "Hello, World" ;

    }

    public SampleClass ( string message)

    {

        greeting = message;

    }

    public void SayHello ()

    {

        System.Console.WriteLine (greeting);

    }

}

class Program

{

    static void Main ( string [] args)

    {

        // Use default constructor.

        SampleClass sampleClass1 = new SampleClass ();

        sampleClass1.SayHello ();

        // Use constructor that takes a string parameter.

        SampleClass sampleClass2 = new SampleClass ( "Hello, Mars" );

       sampleClass2.SayHello ();

    }

}

![clear](Image00021.jpg)

Operator Overloading

Creating different methods with the same name, in the previous example SampleClass () , is called overloading. The compiler knows which method to use because the list of arguments, if there are any, is provided each time an object is created. Overloading can make your code more flexible and readable.

Destructors

You may already know about destructors if you have used C ++. Because of the automatic garbage collection system in C #, it is not likely that you will ever have to implement a destructor unless your class uses unmanaged resources. For more information, see Destructors (C # Programming Guide).

Method overloading

Overloading is the creation of different methods with the same name ( in the previous example , SampleClass () ). The compiler knows which method to use because each time the object is created, a list of arguments (if any) is provided. Overloading can make your code more flexible and more readable.

![clear](Image00021.jpg) Destructors

If you've worked with C ++, you may already have knowledge of destructors. Because of the automatic garbage collection system in C #, it is unlikely that you will ever need to use a destructor unless the class is using unmanaged resources.

How to: Simulate Default Parameters

This example demonstrates the use of method overloading to simulate default parameters, which is not allowed in C #.

Example

class MyClass

{

    static string myMethod (string precip, string country, string location)

    {

        return string.Format ("The {0} in {1} stays mainly in the {2}.",

            precip, country, location);

    }

    static string myMethod (string precip, string country)

    {

        return myMethod (precip, country, "plain");

    }

    static string myMethod ()

    {

        return myMethod ("rain", "Spain", "plain");

    }

    static void Main (string [] args)

    {

        System.Console.WriteLine (myMethod ());

        System.Console.WriteLine (myMethod ("snow", "Walla Walla"));

    }

}

Copy the class and paste it over Class1 in a console application.

Structs

A struct in C # is similar to a class, but structs lack certain features, such as inheritance. Also, since a struct is a value type, it can typically be created faster than a class. If you have tight loops in which new data structures are being created in large numbers, you should consider using a struct instead of a class. Structs are also used to encapsulate groups of data fields such as the coordinates of a point on a grid, or the dimensions of a rectangle.

Example

This example program defines a struct to store a geographic location. It also overrides the ToString () method to produce a more useful output when displayed in the WriteLine statement. As there are no methods in the struct , there is no advantage in defining it as a class.

| struct GeographicLocation{    private double longitude;    private double latitude;    public GeographicLocation (double longitude, double latitude)    {        this .longitude = longitude;        this .latitude = latitude;    }    public override string ToString ()    {        return System.String.Format ( "Longitude: {0} degrees, Latitude: {1} degrees" , longitude, latitude);    }}class Program{    static void Main ()    {        GeographicLocation Seattle = new GeographicLocation (123, 47);        System.Console.WriteLine ( "Position: {0}" , Seattle.ToString ());    }} |

Output

The output from this example looks like this:

Position: Longitude: 123 degrees, Latitude: 47 degrees

Structures

A structure in C # is similar to a class, but structures are missing some features, such as inheritance. In addition, because a struct is a value type , it can be created faster than a class. If you have a continuous loop, where a large number of new data structures are created, it is recommended to use a structure instead of a class. Structures are used to encapsulate groups of data fields, such as grid point coordinates or rectangle dimensions.

Variables and Constants

A variable represents a numeric or string value or an object of a class. The value that the variable stores may change, but the name stays the same. A variable is one type of field . The following code is a simple example of how to declare an integer variable, assign it a value, and then assign it a new value.

| int x = 1;  // x holds the value 1x = 2;      // now x holds the value 2 |

In C #, variables are declared with a specific data type and a label. If your programming background is in loosely typed languages ​ ​ such as JScript, you are used to using the same "var" type for all variables, but in C # you must specify whether your variable is an int , a float , a byte , a short , or any of the more than 20 different data types. The type specifies, among other things, the exact amount of memory that must be allocated to store the value when the application runs. The C # language enforces certain rules when converting a variable from one type to another.

| int answer = 42;string greeting = "Hello, World!" ;double bigNumber = 1e100;System.Console.WriteLine ( "{0} {1} {2}" , answer, greeting, bigNumber); |

Variables and constants

A variable represents a numeric or string value or a class object. The value stored in the variable may change, but the name remains the same. A variable represents one type of field . The following code is a simple example of declaring an integer variable, assigning a value to it, and then assigning a new value .

int x = 1; // x holds the value 1

x = 2; // now x holds the value 2

In C #, variables are declared with a specific data type and label. If earlier you had to work with weakly typed languages, for example JScript, you are used to using one type "var" for all variables, then in C # you need to specify the type of the variable: int , float , byte , short or another , from more than 20 different data types ... The type indicates, among other things, the exact amount of memory that should be allocated to store the value when the application runs. When converting a variable from one type to another, C # follows certain rules.

int answer = 42;

string greeting = "Hello, World!" ;

double bigNumber = 1e100;

System.Console.WriteLine ( "{0} {1} {2}" , answer, greeting, bigNumber);

Constants

A constant is another type of field. It holds a value that is assigned when the program is compiled, and never changes after that. Constants are declared using the const keyword; they are useful for making your code more legible.

| const int speedLimit = 55;const double pi = 3.14159265358979323846264338327950; |

A readonly variable is like a constant but its value is assigned when a program starts up. This allows you to set the value based on some other condition that you don't know until the program is running. But after that first assignment, the value cannot change again while the program is running.

Built-in Data Types

C # is a strongly-typed language. Before a value can be stored in a variable, the type of the variable must be specified, as in the following examples:

| int a = 1;string s = "Hello";XmlDocument tempDocument = new XmlDocument (); |

Note that the type must be specified both for simple, built-in types such as an int, and for complex or custom types such as XmlDocument.

![clear](Image00021.jpg) Constants

Constant is another type of field. It stores the value assigned when the program is compiled and is never changed after that. Constants are declared with the keyword const ; using them helps improve the readability of your code .

const int speedLimit = 55;

const double pi = 3.14159265358979323846264338327950;

The readonly variable is similar to a constant, but a value is assigned to it when the program starts. This makes it possible to set a value based on some other condition unknown prior to program execution. However, after the first assignment, the value cannot be changed again while the program is running.

Built-in data types

C # is a strongly typed language. The type of a variable must be determined before assigning a value to it, as shown in the following examples.

| int a = 1;string s = "Hello";XmlDocument tempDocument = new XmlDocument (); |

Note that the type must be defined for both simple built-in types, such as int, and complex or custom types, such as XmlDocument.

C # includes support for the following built-in data types:

| Data Type | Range |
| byte | 0 .. 255 |
| sbyte | -128 .. 127 |
| short | -32,768 .. 32,767 |
| ushort | 0 .. 65.535 |
| int | -2,147,483,648 .. 2,147,483,647 |
| uint | 0 .. 4,294,967,295 |
| long | -9,223,372,036,854,775,808 .. 9,223,372,036,854,775,807 |
| ulong | 0 .. 18,446,744,073,709,551,615 |
| float | -3.402823e38 .. 3.402823e38 |
| double | -1.79769313486232e308 .. 1.79769313486232e308 |
| decimal | -79228162514264337593543950335 .. 79228162514264337593543950335 |
| char | A Unicode character. |
| string | A string of Unicode characters. |
| bool | True or False. |
| object | An object. |

These data type names are aliases for predefined types in the System namespace. All these types, with the exception of object and string, are value types.

C # supports the following built-in data types.

| Data type Range           byte 0 .. 255                       sbyte -128 .. 127                   short -32,768 .. 32,767                ushort 0 .. 65.535                    int -2,147,483,648 .. 2,147,483,647                  uint 0 .. 4,294,967,295                     long -9,223,372,036,854,775,808 .. 9,223,372,036,854,775,807           ulong 0 .. 18,446,744,073,709,551,615                 float -3.402823e38 .. -3.402823e38 ..                 double -1.79769313486232e308 .. 1.79769313486232e308         decimal -79228162514264337593543950335 ..                                                               79228162514264337593543950335char A Unicode character .                      string A string of Unicode characters .                bool true or false                          object Object .                         |

These data types are called aliases for the predefined types in the System namespace. All of these types, with the exception of object and string, are value types.

Using Built-in Data Types

Built-in data types are used within a C # program in several ways.

As variables:

| int answer = 42;string greeting = "Hello, World!" ; |

As constants:

| const int speedLimit = 55;const double pi = 3.14159265358979323846264338327950; |

As return values ​ ​ and parameters:

| long CalculateSum (int a, int b){    long result = a + b;    return result;} |

To define your own data types, use Classes , Enumerations or Structs .

Converting Data Types

Converting between data types can be done implicitly, in which the conversion is done automatically by the compiler, or explicitly using a cast, in which the programmer forces the conversion, and assumes the risk of losing information.

For example:

| int i = 0;double d = 0;i = 10;d = i;        // An implicit conversiond = 3.5;i = (int) d;  // An explicit conversion, or "cast" |

Like variables .

int answer = 42;

string greeting = "Hello, World!" ;

Like constants .

const int speedLimit = 55;

const double pi = 3.14159265358979323846264338327950;

As return values ​ ​ and parameters.

Data type conversion

Conversion between data types can be done implicitly, in this case, the conversion is performed automatically by the compiler, or explicitly using a cast, in which case the programmer performs the forced conversion, taking into account the risk of possible data loss.

An example .
